# The oversight band

The programme map splits the 135 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the oversight band: 20 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **NICAP**: National Investigations Committee on Aerial Phenomena

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## U.S. House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform

U.S. House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1927; axes C0 A1 D3; 18 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Standing committee; government-wide subpoena jurisdiction.

### What it is

The U.S. House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform is the House's principal investigative committee, chaired in the 118th and 119th Congresses by Rep. James Comer. Since 2023 it has been the primary congressional venue for sworn public testimony about a concealed UAP retrieval and reverse-engineering program, holding hearings in July 2023, November 2024 and September 2025, and continuing a documentary investigation into AARO through the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, chaired by Rep. Anna Paulina Luna.

### Why it is implicated

Oversight is the institution against which the concealment is measured. The claim on this board is not merely that a program exists but that it has been withheld from Congress; the only way to test that is to watch what happens when a committee with subpoena power asks. The pattern across three hearings and multiple document requests is consistent: witnesses testify under penalty of perjury to firsthand or near-firsthand knowledge, the executive branch responds through public affairs rather than document production, and the committee escalates to written demands. In April 2026 Chairwoman Luna wrote to the Secretary of War stating that 'The Task Force has found responses from AARO, when questioned about UAP sightings and provided data, less than adequate', a sitting subcommittee chair putting on the record that the executive's designated UAP office is not answering. That is an oversight failure documented by the overseer, which is the most probative kind of evidence available for a concealment argument.

### The longer account

Three hearings define the record.

On 26 July 2023 the Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, chaired by Rep. Glenn Grothman, held 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Implications on National Security, Public Safety, and Government Transparency' in 2154 Rayburn. The witnesses were Ryan Graves, Executive Director of Americans for Safe Aerospace; Commander David Fravor, former commanding officer of the Black Aces squadron; and David Grusch, former National Reconnaissance Office representative to the UAP Task Force. Grusch testified under oath that in the course of his official duties he was informed of a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering program to which he was denied access, that he had interviewed dozens of witnesses over several years, and that recoveries included non-human biologics, repeatedly stating he could provide specifics in a secure setting. Fravor described the November 2004 Nimitz encounter. Graves testified on reporting stigma and aviation safety. It was the first time a sworn witness told a House committee, on camera, that the United States operates a concealed retrieval program.

On 13 November 2024 the Subcommittee on Cybersecurity, Information Technology, and Government Innovation and the Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs held a joint hearing, 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth.' Witnesses were Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, USN (Ret.), CEO of Ocean STL Consulting; Luis Elizondo; Michael Gold, former NASA Associate Administrator for Space Policy and Partnerships and member of the NASA UAP Independent Study Team; and Michael Shellenberger, founder of Public. Shellenberger testified to the existence of a claimed program he identified as 'Immaculate Constellation,' described as collecting UAP imagery and sensor data from military personnel and sequestering it from the rest of the government. The full transcript was published as HHRG-118-GO12-Transcript-20241113 and the hearing was printed as CHRG-118hhrg57440. The composition of the witness panel is itself notable: a retired flag officer, a former NASA associate administrator, a former DoD official, and a journalist, three of whom held senior federal positions.

On 9 September 2025 the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, chaired by Rep. Anna Paulina Luna, held 'Restoring Public Trust Through UAP Transparency and Whistleblower Protection' in HVC-210. Witnesses were Jeffrey Nuccetelli (USAF veteran), Chief Alexandro Wiggins, George Knapp, and Dylan Borland (USAF veteran). Luna's announcement stated the hearing would 'analyze the work and effectiveness of DoD's congressionally mandated All-Domain Anomaly Resolution Office (AARO)' and that whistleblowers 'should be able to' provide information on spending, classification and declassification 'without retribution.'

The institutional vehicle matters. The Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets was created in early 2025 under the Oversight Committee, with a portfolio spanning the JFK, RFK and MLK assassinations, COVID origins, 9/11, the Epstein files, and UAP and unidentified submerged objects. Members include Reps. Lauren Boebert, Nancy Mace, Jasmine Crockett and Raja Krishnamoorthi. Placing UAP inside a declassification task force rather than a national security subcommittee is a deliberate reframing: it treats the subject as a records-access problem rather than a threat-assessment problem, which is the correct frame if the hypothesis is concealment.

The investigation did not end with the hearing. A committee press release dated 1 April 2026 announces a letter from Luna to Secretary of War Pete Hegseth requesting specific UAP video files, quoting her: 'On September 9, 2025, the Task Force held a hearing focused on UAP concerns. Whistleblowers informed the Task Force that AARO possesses additional video records of potential UAP sightings.' The release states this is 'part of the Committee's larger investigation into the work and effectiveness of the Department of War's congressionally mandated AARO,' and quotes Luna concluding that AARO's responses have been 'less than adequate' and that 'The presence of UAPs in and around the sensitive airspaces of U.S. military installations poses a threat to the security of the armed forces and their readiness.'

Who matters by name: James Comer (full committee chair), Anna Paulina Luna (task force chair and the most persistent driver), Tim Burchett (co-founder of the UAP caucus and a principal mover of the 2023 hearing), Glenn Grothman (2023 subcommittee chair), Nancy Mace, Eric Burlison, Jared Moskowitz and Robert Garcia on the minority and bipartisan side. What the record shows is a committee that has taken sworn testimony, published transcripts, created a dedicated task force, and escalated to written demands on the Secretary of War. What the record does not show is a single document production from the executive branch responsive to the central claim.

### What it has denied

The Committee is the recipient of denials rather than the issuer. Its own published position, stated by Chairwoman Luna in April 2026, is that AARO's responses to its questions have been 'less than adequate' and that the lack of disclosure 'regarding the very real threat posed by UAPs in and around U.S. restricted airspace is concerning.'

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Has the Committee ever issued a subpoena, as opposed to a request letter, for UAP records? No published subpoena has surfaced.
- Grusch told the Committee he could provide specifics in a SCIF. Did a classified follow-on session occur, and is there a record of it?
- The 'Immaculate Constellation' documentation Shellenberger described has never been authenticated by the Committee. Does the Committee hold a copy?
- What did DoD or AARO respond to Luna's video-file request, and on what date?
- Did the Committee ever obtain the classified annex to the June 2021 ODNI preliminary assessment?
- Why has the Senate Armed Services and Intelligence side never held a comparable open hearing, and does that split reflect a jurisdictional bargain?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 205 of 1138, score 45). 18 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C0/A1/D3.

## NORAD and US Northern Command

NORAD and US Northern Command is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1958; axes C0 A3 D2; 8 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Homeland air defence combatant command.

### What it is

The North American Aerospace Defense Command and US Northern Command, the binational and homeland commands responsible for aerospace warning and control over North America. On this board they appear in two eras that are usually discussed separately: the 1975 northern-tier intrusions, recorded in NORAD's own regional logs, and the post-2022 drone-incursion reporting over US military installations.

### Why it is implicated

Because the same command's own records bracket the subject at both ends. The 24th NORAD Region Senior Director's Log for November 1975 records fighters launched against objects over weapons storage areas and lights extinguishing on their arrival. Fifty years later the same command reports more than 600 drone sightings over US installations since 2022 and stands up a counter-drone demonstration programme. Whatever the objects are in either period, the institution recording them is the one whose job is knowing what is in the airspace.

### The longer account

This node is on the map because of what its records are, not what anyone claims about it.

The 1975 material is primary and contemporaneous. The 24th NORAD Region Senior Director's Log, released over a colonel's signature and stamped as pages of a larger compilation, records calls from Strategic Air Command posts, two F-106s launched, that the fighters did not get down to the objects, and lights extinguishing on fighter arrival. The National Military Command Center memorandum of 29 October 1975 shows the traffic reaching the Chairman, the Joint Staff and the service chiefs, with a general officer personally approving rules of engagement for a National Guard helicopter.

One unresolved detail is carried openly rather than smoothed. The NORAD log names Minot Air Force Station, a radar site, where the CINCNORAD message of 11 November says Minot AFB. Those are different places and the discrepancy matters for which base a claim attaches to. Nothing opened resolves it, so the board states both and attaches the claim to neither silently.

The modern material is of a different character and is kept distinct. Drone reports over installations since 2022 are an acknowledged, funded, operational problem with named programmes attached. The board carries them because the same command is the reporter, and because a reader tracing how official language about unidentified objects has changed will want both ends of the line in one place. It does not assert that the two eras are the same phenomenon.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether Minot Air Force Station or Minot AFB is correct in the 1975 record, which decides a hub attachment.
- The remainder of the OASD FOIA compilation the NORAD log was released within, at least 449 unseen pages.
- Whether any NORAD assessment of the 1975 sequence exists beyond the logs themselves.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 453 of 1138, score 32). 8 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** NORAD continued issuing intercept press releases through 2026, including multiple temporary flight restriction violation intercepts in the Northeast and over New Jersey. Source: NORAD Newsroom press releases, 2026
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE 1975 LOG IS PRIMARY AND CONTEMPORANEOUS. The 24th NORAD Region Senior Director's Log for 7 to 19 November 1975 records calls from Strategic Air Command posts, 'two F-106', that 'fighters did not get down to objects', and lights extinguishing on fighter arrival. ## National Investigations Committee on Aerial Phenomena (NICAP)

National Investigations Committee on Aerial Phenomena (NICAP) is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1956; axes C1 A1 D1; 7 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Civilian UFO body incorporated in the District of Columbia, 1956; 1536 Connecticut Avenue NW.

### What it is

The National Investigations Committee on Aerial Phenomena, a civilian body at 1536 Connecticut Avenue NW in Washington DC. It states its own legal form and year: 'NICAP is a non-profit organization incorporated in the District of Columbia (1956).' It was forming in October 1956 under Thomas Townsend Brown, who told a Scripps-Howard reporter that he was executive vice chairman pro tempore and that the group would elect its nine-man board of governors on 1 November. By 1964 it was directed by Maj. Donald E. Keyhoe with Richard H. Hall as acting director and editor, and it published The UFO Evidence, sending copies to every Member of Congress.

### Why it is implicated

NICAP is the exact boundary this board argues about, staffed from the government side. A private organisation campaigning for hearings the government would not hold, whose board of governors carried a former Director of Central Intelligence and the former Air Force Headquarters monitor of Project Blue Book. Vice Admiral R. H. Hillenkoetter served five years on that board and in 1961 joined a majority of it in urging a Congressional investigation. Dewey Fournet Jr. had been the Pentagon's own liaison to the Dayton project. The organisation is not implicated in concealment; it is implicated in the structure, because it shows senior people moving from the inside of the official effort to the outside of it and then pressing the inside from there. The FBI kept a file on it and the CIA filed its book.

### The longer account

The board narrates this organisation constantly and had never named it as an entity until now, which is itself the argument for its presence. Nearly every legacy-era episode the board carries has a NICAP shape to it: a case pressed publicly by a civilian committee whose officers had held the government's own positions, against an Air Force programme that answered in press releases. The 1964 volume states the charge in print and says where it was sent: the Air Force 'has practiced an intolerable degree of secrecy keeping the public in the dark about the amount and possible significance of UFO evidence', and 'Copies of this report have been sent to all Members of Congress.' That is a civilian body performing an oversight function that Congress was not performing.

What makes the node structural rather than merely historical is the composition of the board of governors. A former Director of Central Intelligence sat on it for five years and joined the campaign for hearings. The former Air Force Headquarters monitor of Project Blue Book, the liaison officer between the Dayton project and the Pentagon, sat on it too. This board spends most of its length tracking people who moved between government programmes and private vehicles; NICAP is the earliest well-documented instance in the legacy era, and the direction of travel is out of the official effort and into public advocacy against it.

The surveillance runs the other way and is equally documented. The FBI opened file 100-35992 on the organisation and recorded its self-description as furnished by its secretary Richard H. Hall to a Special Agent of the Washington Field Office in May 1961. The copy of The UFO Evidence that this board reads is the CIA's own, released as CIA-RDP81R00560R000100010001-0. A civilian committee campaigning for hearings had a Bureau file and an Agency reading copy. Both facts come from the government's own paper.

### What it has denied

NICAP issues no denials. It receives none either, in the sense the board means: the denial link on this node has no direction the record establishes. What is documented is the charge running from the civilian side, in print, addressed to Congress. The 1964 volume states that the Air Force 'has practiced an intolerable degree of secrecy keeping the public in the dark about the amount and possible significance of UFO evidence', and the same volume records that copies went to every Member of Congress. The board carries the accusation as published text and declines to convert it into a directed denial by the Air Force, because no Air Force response is in the record opened here.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- The District of Columbia corporate register was not opened. The incorporation and its 1956 date rest on NICAP's own statement of them in the 1964 volume, and nothing independent confirms either.
- The board holds no end date for the organisation and none for Keyhoe's directorship. The node runs open because nothing opened here closes it.
- The custody of the NICAP case files rests on the National UFO Historical Records Center's own account of its collections, which is why that link is Tier 2. No inventory, deed of gift or transfer record has been seen.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 325 of 1138, score 38). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A1/D1.

Hillenkoetter joined with a majority of the NICAP Board in urging a Congressional investigation of the UFO problem.' # 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## Congressional oversight of special access programs

Congressional oversight of special access programs is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1988; axes C0 A3 D3; 13 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: The statutory oversight mechanism, and the hole in it.

### What it is

The congressional oversight apparatus for the legacy program question spans four venues with mismatched authorities: the House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform (broad jurisdiction, no SAP access), the House and Senate Armed Services and Appropriations committees (SAP access, narrow appetite), the two intelligence committees (Controlled Access Program access), and the twelve-member chairman-and-ranking-member channel through which waived Special Access Programs are reported under 10 U.S.C. 119(e). Since 2021 Congress has legislated repeatedly and specifically against concealment, and has been told, repeatedly and specifically, that there is nothing to conceal.

### Why it is implicated

Congress is not a bystander in this record; it is the party that wrote into permanent law the demand that the Executive account for 'any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress.' That sentence exists in the United States Code because members with access to classified briefings concluded such programs might exist. Congress then legislated that AARO must enumerate every instance in which data was withheld from it on classification grounds, barred contractors from charging UAP-related independent research and development to overhead unless the material is furnished to Congress, and created a statutory UAP Records Collection at the National Archives. Each of those provisions is a countermeasure, and countermeasures are designed against something. What Congress has not obtained is compliance: the eminent-domain and Review Board provisions were stripped in conference, the annual SAP report requirement lapsed on 31 December 2021, and in September 2025 the chairwoman of the investigating task force told the public that DoD would not read her members in at all.

### The longer account

Congressional oversight of the legacy program problem has a structural defect that no amount of legislative will has cured: the committees with the appetite lack the access, and the committees with the access lack the appetite.

The modern sequence begins on 26 July 2023, when the House Oversight Committee's National Security subcommittee convened 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Implications on National Security, Public Safety, and Government Transparency.' Former National Reconnaissance Office and National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency officer David Grusch testified under oath alongside former Navy pilot Ryan Graves and retired Navy Commander David Fravor. Grusch alleged a decades-long crash-retrieval and reverse-engineering effort, recovery of non-human biologics, and misappropriation of funds to evade congressional oversight, and said the specifics required a classified setting. The hearing produced a printed transcript, a legislative wave, and no records.

The legislative wave crested in the FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act. Senators Chuck Schumer and Mike Rounds had drafted the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act, which passed the Senate with three teeth: a presidentially appointed nine-member Review Board, a Controlled Disclosure Campaign Plan, and federal eminent domain over recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence held by private parties. Conference removed all three. What survived became sections 1841 through 1843 of Public Law 118-31, codified at 44 U.S.C. 2107 note: a UAP Records Collection at the National Archives, populated by 'copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence,' housed by NARA in Record Group 615. The eminent domain provision, the only clause that would have compelled a private contractor to surrender physical material, was the one the private contractors did not have to face.

What survived elsewhere in the same law is less discussed and arguably sharper. Section 1687(b) of P.L. 118-31 provides that, consistent with DoDI 3204.01, 'independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena shall not be allowable as indirect expenses' on covered contracts 'unless such material and information is made available the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership.' Congress legislated, in the FY2024 NDAA, against a specific accounting mechanism by which a defense contractor could fund UAP work with taxpayer money routed through overhead and owe nobody an explanation. That provision is a fingerprint.

The permanent statute carries more. 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(4) requires the AARO Director, every briefing period, to give the chairs and ranking members of the Armed Services and Intelligence committees 'an enumeration of any instances in which data relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena was not provided to the Office because of classification restrictions on that data or for any other reason.' Congress anticipated that its own office would be stonewalled by other parts of the Executive and built a tripwire. Section 3373(j) requires the Historical Record Report to reach back to 1 January 1945 and to itemize 'any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress,' together with 'any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect unclassified or classified information.' The Comptroller General was directed by section 6803 of P.L. 117-263 to audit that very report process with cleared GAO personnel and to brief Congress semiannually on it.

Congress kept legislating. Public Law 119-60, enacted 18 December 2025, amended 50 U.S.C. 3373(f)(1)(A) so that the data-sharing mandate binds every component of the Department of Defense, not merely elements of the intelligence community, an amendment that would be pointless unless DoD components had been withholding under the old text. The same act added subsection (l)(5), requiring briefings to include details of NORAD and USNORTHCOM intercepts of UAP, with the first post-enactment briefing to reach back to 1 January 2004. Section 1673(a) ordered the AARO Director to make, within 180 days, 'an accounting of security classification guides that apply to information used for reports and investigations of unidentified anomalous phenomena.' Congress is now demanding an inventory of the classification instruments themselves.

Against all of this stands the access problem. On 11 February 2025 Chairman James Comer created the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets under Rep. Anna Paulina Luna, authorized for six months under Committee Rules. On 9 September 2025 Luna told the public that DoD had informed her staff that only HASC and HAC-D members could be read into the relevant SAPs, and that any other Member required the approval of the chairman and ranking member of both. She added: 'Independent SAP oversight has presented a consistent problem for Congress as the program's budgets are classified.' That is the architecture of 10 U.S.C. 119 operating exactly as designed, against the committee investigating it.

### What it has denied

Congress as an institution issues no denial; individual members and the Executive branch witnesses before them do. The operative denial in this lane is structural: DoD's response to congressional demands for UAP SAP access has been to invoke SAP read-in rules rather than to state that no such program exists, a posture Luna characterized on the record on 9 September 2025.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Has AARO ever actually delivered the 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(4) enumeration of data withheld from it on classification grounds, and what did it contain? No unclassified version has surfaced.
- Did any defense contractor ever charge UAP-related IRAD to overhead prior to the FY2024 NDAA §1687(b) prohibition, and did any contracting officer disallow such charges afterward?
- Was the removal of eminent domain and the Review Board from the FY2024 NDAA driven by identifiable members, by contractor lobbying, or by Executive branch objection? The conference record does not attribute it.
- Which Members, if any, have been read into UAP-relevant waived SAPs through the 10 U.S.C. 119(e) twelve-member channel since 2021?
- Was the Task Force's six-month authorization renewed, and under what committee rule, given that it was still transmitting letters through Burlison in May 2026?
- Did GAO's cleared auditors identify compliance failures in AARO's historical record report process, and were those findings ever briefed in unclassified form?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 252 of 1138, score 42). 13 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A3/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(4) requires the AARO Director each briefing period to provide the chairs and ranking members of the Armed Services and Intelligence committees 'an enumeration of any instances in which data relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena was not provided to the Office because of classification restrictions on that data or for any other reason.' Source: 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(4), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29

Source: DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024
- **Tier 1 documented.** On 26 July 2023 the House Oversight Committee held 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Implications on National Security, Public Safety, and Government Transparency,' with sworn testimony from David Grusch, Ryan Graves and David Fravor; the hearing transcript was printed as HHRG-118-GO06. Source: U.S. House of Representatives, Committee on Oversight and Accountability, hearing transcript HHRG-118-GO06-Transcript-20230726, https://www.congress.gov/118/meeting/house/116282/documents/HHRG-118-GO06-Transcript-20230726.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** On 9 September 2025 Chairwoman Luna stated that DoD restricted UAP SAP read-ins to HASC and HAC-D members and that 'Independent SAP oversight has presented a consistent problem for Congress as the program's budgets are classified. Additionally, oversight reporting to Congress is classified, and is only provided to the authorizing and appropriations committees of jurisdiction.' Source: House Oversight, 'Luna Opens Hearing on Restoring Public Trust Through UAP Transparency,' 9 September 2025, https://oversight.house.gov/release/luna-opens-hearing-on-restoring-public-trust-through-uap-transparency
- **Tier 1 documented.** Public Law 119-60 §1671 added 50 U.S.C. 3373(l)(5), requiring semiannual briefings to detail NORAD and USNORTHCOM UAP intercepts, with the first post-enactment briefing covering intercepts back to 1 January 2004 not previously provided. Source: Pub. L. 119-60, div. A, title XVI, §1671(a)-(b), 18 December 2025, 139 Stat. 1201-1202, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29
- **Tier 1 documented.** Public Law 119-60 §1673(a) required the AARO Director, within 180 days of 18 December 2025, to make 'an accounting of security classification guides that apply to information used for reports and investigations of unidentified anomalous phenomena.' Source: Pub. L. 119-60, div. A, title XVI, §1673(a), 139 Stat. 1202, note to 50 U.S.C. 3373, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29
- **Tier 1 documented.** Public Law 119-60, enacted 18 December 2025, amended 50 U.S.C. 3373(f)(1)(A) to require that each component of the Department of Defense, not only each element of the intelligence community, make UAP data immediately available to AARO. Source: Pub. L. 119-60, div. A, title XVI, §1672(b), 18 December 2025, 139 Stat. 1202; amendment note to 50 U.S.C. 3373, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1687(b) of Public Law 118-31 provides that independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena 'shall not be allowable as indirect expenses' on covered DoD contracts unless the material and information is made available to the congressional defense and intelligence committees and congressional leadership. Source: Pub. L. 118-31, div. A, title XVI, §1687(b), 22 December 2023, 137 Stat. 621, set out as a note under 50 U.S.C. 3373, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 6803 of Public Law 117-263 directed the Comptroller General to identify cleared GAO personnel to audit the AARO historical record report process, including on-site work, and to brief the intelligence committees, defense committees and congressional leadership semiannually on AARO's progress and legislative compliance. Source: Pub. L. 117-263, div. F, title LXVIII, §6803, 23 December 2022, 136 Stat. 3594, as amended by Pub. L. 118-159 §6802, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 NDAA (Public Law 118-31), codified at 44 U.S.C. 2107 note, require identification, review, organization and transmission of UAP records to NARA for inclusion in the UAP Records Collection, which NARA maintains as Record Group 615. Source: Rep. Eric Burlison to MIT Lincoln Laboratory, 7 May 2026, citing sections 1841-1843 and Record Group 615, https://burlison.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/burlison.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/mit-lincoln-lab_uap-tape-letter.pdf; NARA guidance at https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/uap-guidance
- **Tier 1 documented.** The 10 U.S.C. 119 annual report to the defense committees enumerating every DoD special access program, its cost history and milestones, terminated effective 31 December 2021 under section 1061 of Public Law 114-328. Source: 10 U.S.C. 119, Statutory Notes, 'Termination of Reporting Requirements', https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** Where the Secretary of Defense waives SAP reporting, the statute requires the information and the justification to be provided 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees' rather than to the full committees. Source: 10 U.S.C. 119
- **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** At that hearing Grusch alleged a concealed multi-decade UAP crash-retrieval and reverse-engineering program, recovery of 'non-human biologics,' and misappropriation of funds to evade congressional oversight. Source: Roll Call, 'Whistleblower at UAP hearing claims US found non-human remains,' 26 July 2023, https://rollcall.com/2023/07/26/whistleblower-at-uap-hearing-claims-non-human-remains/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Schumer and Rounds reintroduced the UAP Disclosure Act as Senate Amendment 2610 to S.4638 in the 118th Congress after the FY2024 conference strip-out, and a further version was offered as Senate Amendment 3111 to S.2296 in the 119th Congress. Source: Congress.gov, S.Amdt.2610 to S.4638, 118th Congress, https://www.congress.gov/amendment/118th-congress/senate-amendment/2610/text; S.Amdt.3111 to S.2296, 119th Congress, https://www.congress.gov/amendment/119th-congress/senate-amendment/3111/text
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The Senate-passed UAP Disclosure Act provisions establishing an independent nine-member Review Board, a Review Board staff, federal eminent domain over recovered technologies of unknown origin held by private parties, and a controlled disclosure process were removed by the FY2024 NDAA conference committee and are not in the enacted law. Source: Douglas Dean Johnson, 'Quick guide to UAP-related provisions in the final FY 2024 National Defense Authorization Act', https://douglasjohnson.ghost.io/quick-guide-to-uap-related-provisions-in-the-final-fy-2024-national-defense-authorization-act/; Covington, 'Implications of the UAP Amendment in the 2024 NDAA', https://www.insidegovernmentcontracts.com/2024/01/implications-of-the-unidentified-anomalous-phenomena-uap-amendment-in-the-2024-national-defense-authorization-act-ndaa/

- Congressional oversight of special access programs comes in from **United States Department of Defense (secondary designation: Department of War)** (institution, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. ## Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities (Church Committee)

Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities (Church Committee) is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1975-01-27 to 1976-04-26; axes C2 A1 D3; 7 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Established by S. Res. 21, 27 January 1975; final report S. Rept. 94-755, six books, 1976.

### What it is

The Senate Select Committee to Study Governmental Operations with Respect to Intelligence Activities, established by Senate Resolution 21 on 27 January 1975 'to conduct an investigation and study of governmental operations with respect to intelligence activities and of the extent, if any, to which illegal, improper, or unethical activities were engaged in by any agency of the Federal Government.' Eleven senators, six from the majority and five from the minority, chaired by Frank Church of Idaho with John G. Tower of Texas as Vice Chairman. A staff of 100, including 60 professionals, conducted more than 800 interviews and over 250 executive hearings across documentation in excess of 110,000 pages. Book I of the final report, Senate Report 94-755, was filed on 26 April 1976.

### Why it is implicated

It is the benchmark, and it is the benchmark's own limit case. Two speakers in the Michels corpus who agree on almost nothing else, Michael Shellenberger and Mick West, each name this committee as the model for what congressional penetration of a compartmented programme should look like. The committee's report describes a penetration that was mediated at every step. It never once examined an agency's files on its own. The CIA and other executive agencies limited its access to the full record, in some instances by asserting executive privilege. And three chapters of its findings, one of them the chapter on budgetary oversight, were deleted from the published report at the request of the agencies it was investigating. The most thorough inquiry ever made into the American intelligence system reported that it could not see the files and could not publish what it found about the money.

### The longer account

The committee is carried here for what it concedes rather than for what it achieved, and the concession is in its own words: 'Despite its legal Senate mandate, and the issuance of subpoenas, in no instance has the Committee been able to examine the agencies' files on its own. In all the agencies, whether CIA, FBI, NSA, INR, DIA, or the NSC, documents and evidence have been presented through the filter of the agency itself.' That sentence is the sharpest statement on this board of the structural problem every subsequent oversight claim inherits. Subpoena power did not produce unmediated access. What reached the Senate was what the holder of the file elected to hand over.

The money follows the same pattern twice. Among the committee's findings: 'Congress' failure as a whole to monitor the intelligence agencies' expenditures has been a major element in the ineffective legislative oversight of the intelligence community,' and more broadly that 'Mechanisms for, and the practice of, congressional oversight have not been adequate.' The committee recommended annual publication of the aggregate national intelligence figure. Then the published report's own note records that 'At the request of the agencies, the Committee deleted three chapters from this report: "Cover," "Espionage," and "Budgetary Oversight."' The chapter on budgetary oversight was removed at the request of the agencies whose budgets it examined. Two sections of the covert-action chapter and one section of the State Department chapter went the same way.

The board uses this node as a calibration point. When a witness says a compartmented programme can be reached by a determined congressional committee, this is the strongest instance available, and it is documented failing on exactly the two axes that matter to this board: file access and money. What it did produce was permanent. It recommended the standing oversight committee that followed it, and the record of its own obstruction survives in the published volumes because the committee chose to print the obstruction alongside the findings.

### What it has denied

The board carries one denial link on this node and does not assert its direction, because the record does not establish one. What the record does establish is a self-report: the committee's own final report names the Central Intelligence Agency and other executive-branch agencies as having limited its access to the full record, in some instances by asserting executive privilege, and records that three chapters were deleted at the agencies' request. That is a committee describing what was done to it, published in the document the agencies reviewed before it went out. The board treats it as a documented behaviour with the direction left open, rather than converting a self-report into a directed act.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The three deleted chapters, 'Cover', 'Espionage' and 'Budgetary Oversight', have not been located in any later release. The board knows they existed and knows who asked for their removal, and holds none of their text.
- The board reads Book I directly and Book III through the Angleton citations. Books II, IV, V and VI have not been opened for this node.
- The succession to the permanent Senate Select Committee on Intelligence is graded Tier 2 because the establishing resolution was not opened. The committee's own report refers throughout to 'any successor oversight committee', which is intent, not enactment.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 212 of 1138, score 45). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A1/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 'On January 27, 1975, Senate Resolution 21 established a select committee "to conduct an investigation and study of governmental operations with respect to intelligence activities and of the extent, if any, to which illegal, improper, or unethical activities were engaged in by any agency of the Federal Government."' Book I of the final report, Senate Report 94-755, was filed on 'APRIL 26 (legislative day, APRIL 14), 1976'. Source: S. Rept. 94-755, Book I, title page and Part I section A, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/sites-default-files-94755-i.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Among its findings: 'The Committee finds that Congress has failed to provide the necessary statutory guidelines to ensure that intelligence agencies carry out their missions in accord with constitutional processes. Mechanisms for, and the practice of, congressional oversight have not been adequate.' And on money: 'Congress' failure as a whole to monitor the intelligence agencies' expenditures has been a major element in the ineffective legislative oversight of the intelligence community.' Source: S. Rept. 94-755, Book I, Findings and Recommendations, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/sites-default-files-94755-i.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The committee was chaired by Frank Church of Idaho with John G. Tower of Texas as Vice Chairman, and had eleven members, six from the majority and five from the minority. 'The Committee staff of 100, including 60 professionals, has assisted the 11 members of the Committee in this indepth inquiry which involved more than 800 interviews, over 250 executive hearings, and documentation in excess of 110,000 pages.' Source: S. Rept. 94-755, Book I, committee page and Part I section C, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/sites-default-files-94755-i.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The committee's own account of the obstacles it met: 'Despite its legal Senate mandate, and the issuance of subpoenas, in no instance has the Committee been able to examine the agencies' files on its own. In all the agencies, whether CIA, FBI, NSA, INR, DIA, or the NSC, documents and evidence have been presented through the filter of the agency itself.' It adds that 'the Central Intelligence Agency and other agencies of the executive branch have limited the Committee's access to the full record.' Source: S. Rept. 94-755, Book I, Part I section C, 'The Focus and Scope of the Committee's Inquiry and Obstacles Encountered', https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/sites-default-files-94755-i.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The report's own publication note records that it 'has been reviewed and declassified by the appropriate executive agencies' and that 'At the request of the agencies, the Committee deleted three chapters from this report: "Cover," "Espionage," and "Budgetary Oversight."' Two sections of the covert-action chapter and one section of the State Department chapter were also deleted at agency request. Source: S. Rept. 94-755, Book I, NOTE following the Letter of Transmittal, https://www.intelligence.senate.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/08/sites-default-files-94755-i.pdf
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Two Michels-corpus speakers who agree on very little else, Michael Shellenberger and Mick West, each name the Church Committee as the model for what oversight of a compartmented programme should look like. The committee's own report describes an inquiry whose every document arrived through the filter of the agency that held it. Source: michels-corpus-02.md file 02 at 13;52 and 14;51 (Shellenberger); michels-corpus-05.md file 04 at 18;41 (West)

## GAO/NSIAD-88-152 - Special Access Programs: DOD Criteria and Procedures for Creating Them Need Improvement

GAO/NSIAD-88-152 - Special Access Programs: DOD Criteria and Procedures for Creating Them Need Improvement is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1988-05-24; axes C1 A1 D1; 6 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Unclassified summary to congressional committees, B-230522, 24 May 1988; summary of a classified report of 1 April 1988.

### What it is

GAO's unclassified summary to the four defence committees, B-230522, 24 May 1988, of a classified report of 1 April 1988 on how the Department of Defense decides to make a programme special access. It answers section 1133 of Public Law 100-180.

### Why it is implicated

It is the record behind this board's argument that special access status is asserted rather than justified. GAO found that DoD does not adequately follow its own criteria, that the definition of a special access programme is not clear inside the department, and that the official required to hold a list of all such programmes does not hold one. It also dates the Army's own central oversight office to September 1984, ten years before the reform this board carries at dod-sap-reform-1994.

### The longer account

The board's oversight case has always turned on a simple claim: that the special access system is not policed by the department that runs it. This report is Congress's own auditor saying so in 1988, in an unclassified summary of a classified report, to Les Aspin, Bill Chappell Jr., Sam Nunn and John C. Stennis. Three findings carry. First, creation authority is narrow and named: only the three service secretaries and the Deputy Under Secretary of Defense for Policy may establish a special access programme. Second, nobody held the list: the Deputy Under Secretary for Policy was required to maintain a listing of all special access programmes and did not, on security grounds, and the Secretary's office had no centralized office with cognizance of all of them. Third, and this is the finding that moves two existing nodes, the reform chain starts earlier than the board had it: a DoD review of Army special access programs in 1983 revealed systemic weaknesses which resulted in the establishment of a central oversight office in September 1984. The January 1994 dates on dod-sap-reform-1994 and sapoc stand; what changes is that they are no longer the beginning.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The classified report of 1 April 1988 that this document summarises.
- GAO/NSIAD-89-133 of 4 May 1989 is opened and not yet built as a node; it carries the carve-out contract growth from 63 in 1971 to 1,061 in 1987.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 437 of 1138, score 33). 6 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A1/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO's central finding: 'We found that the Department of Defense does not adequately follow criteria to be used in determining the need to establish programs as special accessrequired. The definition of special accessprograms is not clear within the Department of Defense and, until recently, the oversight role of the Office of the Secretary of Defense in the programs' creation has been limited.' - **Tier 1 documented.** Nobody held a list: 'The Deputy Under Secretary of Defense for Policy is required to maintain a listing of all special access programs, but does not do so because of security concerns.' GAO adds that 'the Secretary's Office has no centralized office with cognizance of all special access programs'. ## Senate Select Committee on Intelligence

Senate Select Committee on Intelligence is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1976; axes C2 A3 D2; 6 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: SSCI; author of the IAA UAP provisions.

### What it is

The Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, whose annual Intelligence Authorization Act has carried the sharpest UAP language of the modern period: Secs. 6802 and 6803 of the FY2023 IAA, Sec. 1102 of the FY2024 IAA and its enacted remnant Sec. 7343, and Secs. 6801 and 6802 of the FY2025 IAA.

### Why it is implicated

It is where the strongest legislative language on this subject originates and where it dies, and the members entitled to the most compartmented briefings are the ones who wrote the hardest provisions. It is also, on the record opened in this corpus, the body a CIA component was advised to inform about recovered MKULTRA records before informing the requester's own attorneys.

### The longer account

The committee reaches this map twice over. Its modern record is legislative: successive Intelligence Authorization Acts carrying UAP provisions, a chairman who authored the contractor turn-in and a vice chairman who cosponsored the eminent domain text, and then the FY2026 Act, Division F of P.L. 119-60, carrying none. The corpus opened here adds a much older instance of the same committee at the same hinge. When CIA's Office of Technical Service found seven boxes of MKULTRA finance folders in 1977 in response to John Marks's Freedom of Information Act request, the memorandum recommending what to do about it set the order of notification: inform the Senate Select Committee of the existence of the recently located records prior to informing Mr. Marks's attorneys. Six weeks later the material reached the public through this committee's own joint hearing of 3 August 1977. That is the committee functioning as the route by which a compartment's surviving paper became public, which is the function the board is testing it against now.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Why the FY2026 Intelligence Authorization Act carries no UAP provision.
- Whether the committee holds any record of the 1977 notification beyond the hearing print.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 367 of 1138, score 36). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** SSCI Chairman Warner authored the contractor turn-in and Vice Chairman Rubio cosponsored the eminent domain amendment, both filed 13 July 2023. Source: Congressional Record, 13 July 2023 (SA 666 and SA 797), GPO
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2023 (Secs. 6802, 6803), FY2024 (Sec. 1102 Senate / Sec. 7343 enacted) and FY2025 (Secs. 6801, 6802) all carried UAP provisions. Source: P.L. 117-263; S. 2226 as engrossed Division M; P.L. 118-31; P.L. 118-159, GPO
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2026, Division F of P.L. 119-60, contains no UAP provisions. Source: Full-text search of P.L. 119-60 Division F, GPO

# 2000-2016: AAWSAP / AATIP

## Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information

Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2009-12-29; axes C0 A3 D3; 7 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: executive-order.

### What it is

Executive Order 13526, Classified National Security Information, signed on 29 December 2009 and published at 75 FR 707. It is the operative classification order and it is where special access programs come from. Section 4.3(a) restricts creation of a SAP to six officials or their principal deputies, the Secretaries of State, Defense, Energy and Homeland Security, the Attorney General and the Director of National Intelligence, and assigns intelligence SAPs to the DNI. Section 4.3(b) sets the internal rules: numbers kept at an absolute minimum, an accounting system in each agency, annual review, a briefing to the National Security Advisor on request, and access for the Director of the Information Security Oversight Office that the agency head may narrow. Section 4.3(c) subordinates the section to 10 U.S.C. 119, and section 6.2(a) subordinates the order to the Atomic Energy Act for Restricted Data.

### Why it is implicated

Creation is executive and unilateral; reporting is statutory and comes afterward. Congress has never legislated over the authority to establish a special access program, so the six creating officials can stand one up with no statutory basis at all and 10 U.S.C. 119 only tells them what to report once it exists. The order is therefore the instrument that makes the central structural claim on this board possible rather than merely alleged, and it is the instrument agencies cite when they refuse to answer: two FOIA refusals carried here rest on it, USNORTHCOM in 2013 at section 1.4c and the Space Force in 2023 at section 1.1(a).

### The longer account

The provision that matters most is the one that reads like housekeeping. Section 4.3(b)(3) grants the Director of ISOO access to special access programs and in the same breath says that 'An agency head may limit access to a special access program to the Director of the Information Security Oversight Office and no more than one other employee of the Information Security Oversight Office or, for special access programs that are extraordinarily sensitive and vulnerable, to the Director only.' The executive branch's own oversight body for the entire classification system can lawfully be reduced to a single individual, at the discretion of the person being overseen.

That is one of three narrowings, and the board's reading is that they are independent of each other. 10 U.S.C. 119(e) reduces the congressional readership of a waived SAP to twelve members. Section 4.3(b)(3) can reduce ISOO to one person. 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) relieves the Director of the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency of industrial-security oversight on a carve-out. Congressional, executive and industrial-security oversight are each narrowed by a different authority, so closing any one of them would leave the other two intact.

The order also contains the provisions a whistleblower would reach for, and they have no teeth inside the order itself. Section 1.7(a) forbids classification to conceal violations of law, inefficiency or administrative error, to prevent embarrassment, to restrain competition or to delay release of information that does not require protection; section 1.7(b) forbids classifying basic scientific research not clearly related to national security. Neither carries an enforcement mechanism in the text. The only channels are the section 5.3 appeal process and the intelligence-community whistleblower statutes.

### What it has denied

An executive order does not deny; it is the instrument cited in denials, which is a different and more useful thing to be able to show. Two refusals on this board rest on it directly: USNORTHCOM's FOIA response in case FY14-19NOV2013-07, citing section 1.4c, and the Space Force position reported in December 2023 citing section 1.1(a). Both concern fastwalker and slowwalker material, both are exemption claims rather than assertions of non-existence, and both trace to the same document. The order's own section 1.7 makes several of the motives a reader might suspect impermissible on the face of the text, and supplies no way to test whether they were present.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- How many special access programs exist is not established by anything on this board. The order requires each agency to keep an accounting system; no aggregate from one has been located.
- Whether the President has ever exercised the 'unless otherwise authorized by the President' clause in sec. 4.3(a), or directed that ISOO be denied access to a specific programme, is not established. The board holds no document either way.
- The annual reviews required by sec. 4.3(b)(4) and the on-request briefings under (b)(5) produce records by implication. None has been located, and no request for them is logged here.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 60 of 1138, score 75). 9 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** E.O. 13526 sec. 4.3 requires agency heads to keep the number of special access programs at an absolute minimum and to review each program annually to determine whether it continues to meet the criteria for creation. Source: Executive Order 13526, sec. 4.3
- **Tier 1 documented.** E.O. 13526 sec. 4.3(a) provides that for programs pertaining to intelligence activities, including special activities, or intelligence sources or methods, this function shall be exercised by the Director of National Intelligence. Source: Executive Order 13526, sec. 4.3(a)
- **Tier 1 documented.** E.O. 13526 sec. 4.3(a): 'Unless otherwise authorized by the President, only the Secretaries of State, Defense, Energy, and Homeland Security, the Attorney General, and the Director of National Intelligence, or the principal deputy of each, may create a special access program.' Source: Executive Order 13526, sec. 4.3(a)

An agency head may limit access to a special access program to the Director of the Information Security Oversight Office and no more than one other employee of the Information Security Oversight Office or, for special access programs that are extraordinarily sensitive and vulnerable, to the Director only.' The executive-branch oversight body for the entire classification system can lawfully be reduced to a single individual for the most sensitive SAPs. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2010-01-05/html/E9-31418.htm

### Its strongest connections

- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information runs out to **10 U.S.C. 119 - Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight** (legislation, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Sec. 4.3(c): nothing in the order supersedes any requirement made by or under 10 U.S.C. 119. Sources: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2010-01-05/html/E9-31418.htm, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2010-01-05/html/E9-31418.htm
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information runs out to **10 U.S.C. 119 - Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight** (legislation, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Creates the programs the statute then reports on. E.O. 13526 sec. 4.3 is the source of authority to create a SAP; 10 U.S.C. 119 only prescribes what must be told to Congress afterwards. Sources: Executive Order 13526, Classified National Security Information, https://www.archives.gov/isoo/policy-documents/cnsi-eo.html, dated 2009-12-29; 10 U.S.C. 119, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-12.
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information runs out to **10 U.S.C. 119 - Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight** (legislation, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: creates what the statute must report. The order creates the object the statute reports on. EO 13526 sec. 4.3(a) is the authority to establish a special access program at all, and restricts it to six officials or their principal deputies: the Secretaries of State, Defense, Energy and Homeland Security, the Attorney General, and the Director of National Intelligence. 10 U.S.C. 119 then obliges the Secretary of Defense to report the DoD ones to Congress. Creation is executive and unilateral; reporting is statutory. Both texts were read on 22 August 2026, and neither cites the other - the relationship is that one supplies the programmes the other counts. Sources: Executive Order 13526, Classified National Security Information, 75 FR 707, 5 January 2010, sec. 4.3 (read from govinfo, 22 August 2026), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2010-01-05/html/E9-31418.htm, dated 2026-08-22; 10 U.S.C. 119 (OLRC prelim, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22.
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information runs out to **Acknowledged, unacknowledged and waived - the three states of a DoD SAP** (domain, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: All SAP categories descend from a single executive order provision. Section 4.3(a) names the six officials who may create a SAP; the DoD taxonomy of acknowledged, unacknowledged and waived operates entirely below that grant. Sources: Executive Order 13526, Classified National Security Information, https://www.archives.gov/isoo/policy-documents/cnsi-eo.html, dated 2009-12-29; DoD Directive 5205.07, 1 July 2010, Change 1 31 August 2018, https://web.archive.org/web/20190922210932/https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2018-08-31.
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information comes in from **ICD 906 - Controlled Access Programs (ODNI)** (document, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: ICD 906 is the DNI's exercise of the section 4.3 SAP authority. ICD 906 section B.1 establishes oversight of IC special access programs 'pursuant to EO 13526, Section 4.3', which assigns intelligence SAPs to the DNI. Sources: Intelligence Community Directive 906, Controlled Access Programs, https://web.archive.org/web/20250112183207/https://www.dni.gov/files/documents/ICD/ICD-906-Controlled-Access-Programs.pdf, dated 2024-12-19; Executive Order 13526, Classified National Security Information, https://www.archives.gov/isoo/policy-documents/cnsi-eo.html, dated 2009-12-29.
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information comes in from **Defense Support Program (DSP) missile early warning satellites** (program, board tier B), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Both refusals rest on the same executive order. Both refusals rest on the same executive order, cited at section 1.4c in 2013 and at section 1.1(a) in 2023. - Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information comes in from **Atomic Energy Act of 1946 (McMahon Act)** (legislation, board tier B), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The category this Act invented is the one the executive order cannot reach. EO 13526 sec. 6.2(a) provides that nothing in the order supersedes any requirement made by or under the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 and that Restricted Data is handled and declassified only under that Act; the 1954 statute carried the category forward from this one. Sources: Executive Order 13526, sec. 6.2(a), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2010-01-05/pdf/E9-31418.pdf, dated 2009-12-29.
- Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information runs out to **Information Security Oversight Office** (institution, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: ISOO access to SAPs is granted and simultaneously conditioned. Section 4.3(b) affords the ISOO Director access to special access programs 'consistent with the policies and procedures directed by the President' - a defeasible right. - Executive Order 13526 - Classified National Security Information comes in from **FY2024 IAA sec. 7606(c) - Agency Review of Special Access Programs** (legislation, board tier C), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Requires a personnel census from each of the six SAP-creating officials. FY2024 IAA sec. 7606(c) addresses 'each head of an agency who is authorized to establish a special access program by Executive Order 13526', due within one year. # 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## AARO Report on the Historical Record of U.S. Government Involvement with UAP, Volume I

AARO Report on the Historical Record of U.S. Government Involvement with UAP, Volume I is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024; axes C1 A3 D3; 32 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: report.

### What it is

The Report on the Historical Record of U.S. Government Involvement with Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena, Volume I, is a 63-page unclassified report published by AARO on 8 March 2024 under a congressional mandate to document the US Government's UAP record back to 1945. It covers the period from 1945 to 31 October 2023 and was largely prepared under director Sean Kirkpatrick before his retirement on 1 December 2023. A Volume II covering 1 November 2023 to 15 April 2024 was promised and has not appeared.

### Why it is implicated

Volume I is the government's formal written denial that a legacy recovery and reverse-engineering programme exists, and as such it is the central artifact of the concealment argument rather than a rebuttal to it. It is an investigation of roughly thirty interviewees conducted without interviewing the most prominent complainant, written by the office whose own conduct several of the allegations concern, and published by the same office that decides which claims are in scope. Its own text concedes that an Intelligence Community Controlled Access Program had its scope expanded in 2021 to protect UAP reverse-engineering, which is the single most consequential sentence any US government UAP document has ever contained. It disposes of the rest of the record by attributing it to circular reporting among a group of people who worked on a real, congressionally funded Defense Intelligence Agency programme. Volume I is on this map because it is the document a concealment effort would have to produce, and because the promised second half has never arrived.

### The longer account

The FY2023 NDAA, enacted December 2022, required AARO to produce a written report on the historical record of the United States Government relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena going back to 1 January 1945. AARO split the deliverable. Volume I would carry findings from 1945 to 31 October 2023. Volume II would carry anything derived from interviews and research completed between 1 November 2023 and 15 April 2024, on a date set by section 6802 of the FY2023 NDAA. Volume I went to Congress in the first week of March 2024 and to the public on 8 March. Volume II has not been published.

The methodology is stated plainly in the report and is the first thing a hostile reader should notice. AARO reviewed official investigatory efforts since 1945, researched classified and unclassified archives, partnered with IC and DoD officials responsible for controlled and special access programme oversight, and conducted approximately 30 interviews. Thirty interviews is the entire human-source basis for a report covering seventy-eight years. AARO categorised the interviewees into tiers, with Tier 1 being those referred to AARO after speaking with Members of Congress or congressional staff. The report then states that none of the interviewees had firsthand knowledge of these programmes, that they were not approved for access and did not work on these efforts, and that this likely resulted in misinterpretation.

The findings are absolute in tone. AARO found no evidence that any USG investigation, academic-sponsored research, or official review panel has confirmed that any sighting of a UAP represented extraterrestrial technology. AARO found no empirical evidence for claims that the USG and private companies have been reverse-engineering extraterrestrial technology. AARO has found no evidence of any authentic UAP-related NDA or other evidence threatening death or violence for disclosing UAP information. AARO has found no evidence that U.S. companies ever possessed off-world technology; the report says executives, scientists and chief technology officers of named companies met the Director of AARO and denied on the record ever having recovered, possessed or reverse-engineered such technology. A metal sample obtained from a private UAP investigating organisation and the US Army is assessed as a manufactured terrestrial alloy of magnesium, zinc and bismuth with trace elements including lead.

Against that, the report contains admissions its authors evidently regarded as harmless. It states that in 2021, without sufficient justification, the scope of an IC Controlled Access Program was expanded to protect UAP reverse-engineering, that the programme never recovered or reverse-engineered anything, and that it was disestablished for lack of merit. It states that KONA BLUE was a Prospective Special Access Program proposed to the Department of Homeland Security, never approved, whose supporters never provided empirical evidence. It states that on 24 June 2009 Senator Harry Reid wrote to Deputy Secretary of Defense William Lynn III asking that AAWSAP/AATIP be made a DoD Special Access Program and that Lynn declined on the recommendation of Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence James Clapper. It states that a named former CIA official signed a memo rejecting the claim that he had managed the movement of and experimentation on off-world technology, which means AARO went to that official and obtained a denial rather than a record. It states that an alleged 1961 Special National Intelligence Estimate circulating online is inauthentic.

The report's explanatory theory is that the modern allegations are, in large part, the result of circular reporting from a group of individuals who believe this to be the case despite the lack of any evidence, and that these individuals have been involved in various UAP-related endeavours since at least 2009, many of them connected to the cancelled DIA programme and the failed attempt to re-establish it under DHS as KONA BLUE. This is a genetic argument. It explains the allegations by the identity of the people making them rather than by testing what they allege, and it does so while conceding that the DIA programme was real, congressionally funded, and terminated over the objections of a sitting Senate Majority Leader.

The report also records lines of effort that are useful to this board regardless of its conclusions. AARO partnered with the archives of the Defense Intelligence Agency, the Central Intelligence Agency, the Department of Energy, the National Security Agency, the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency, the Services and the Combatant Commands, and with the US National Archives on locating UAP data. It interviewed five former Air Force members who served in and around intercontinental ballistic missile silos at Malmstrom, Ellsworth and other sites regarding a narrative of UAP over nuclear facilities. It records Captain Edward Ruppelt of Project GRUDGE noting concentrations of cases in the Los Alamos and Albuquerque area, Oak Ridge, White Sands, Strategic Air Command locations, ports and industrial sites.

One correction this board has already logged belongs here permanently. A full-text search of the released Volume I returns no occurrence of the term YANKEE BLUE. The widely repeated claim that AARO conceded that programme in Volume I does not survive a check of the document.

Volume I should be read as a primary record of what the United States Government said in March 2024, which is exactly the tier at which it belongs. It is not a primary record of the facts it asserts, and its authors have never produced the second half.

### What it has denied

Volume I is itself the denial. It asserts that no US Government investigation has ever confirmed a UAP as extraterrestrial technology, that there is no empirical evidence of reverse-engineering by government or private companies, that no authentic UAP-related non-disclosure agreement exists, that no US company ever possessed off-world technology, and that all named hidden reverse-engineering programmes either do not exist, are misidentified authentic national security programmes, or resolve to an unwarranted and disestablished programme.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Volume II is overdue and unpublished. Its absence is the single largest gap in the government's own historical account and the first thing any oversight body should compel.
- The report does not name the IC Controlled Access Program whose scope was expanded in 2021 to protect UAP reverse-engineering, the official who expanded it, or the official who disestablished it.
- David Grusch, whose allegations prompted the congressional interest that produced the report, was not interviewed for it. The report does not explain the omission.
- The report does not publish the list of roughly thirty interviewees, their tier assignments, or the questions put to them, so the sufficiency of the investigation cannot be independently assessed.
- The report says the executives of named companies denied on the record ever possessing off-world technology, but does not name the companies, publish the denials, or state whether they were taken under oath.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 5 of 1138, score 157). 38 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 18 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D3.
