## The Sol Foundation

The Sol Foundation is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2022; axes C1 A2 D3; 19 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: UAP research and policy non-profit.

### What it is

The Sol Foundation is a private non-profit co-founded by Stanford pathology professor Garry Nolan and anthropologist Peter Skafish that describes its work as science, policy and public education for what it calls the post-UAP world. It runs observational and analytical research projects, publishes a numbered white-paper and policy-briefing series, and holds an annual conference. In June 2026 five people connected to it - both co-founders, a senior adviser, an advisory board member and its production lead - were named to the US government's UAP Science Advisory Council.

### Why it is implicated

Sol is the institution that converted the private technical layer into a policy actor with standing inside government. Its published output is not popular advocacy; it is a policy programme aimed squarely at the concealment mechanism, including a November 2024 paper by its own executive director on executive-branch UAP secrecy and the harm to Congress, a May 2025 paper by retired Colonel Karl Nell calling for US government disclosure, and an April 2026 briefing by its senior legislative affairs officer - a 37-year Senate Armed Services Committee staffer - proposing a whistleblower restitution fund to incentivise disclosures to Congress. Within under three years of formation, that programme placed a plurality of the members of the first standing outside scientific advisory body the US government has convened on this subject. The single largest gap in the Sol record is that it names no donors, and the board treats an unfunded map of a funding question as an open problem rather than a settled one.

### The longer account

The Sol Foundation is the closest thing the modern disclosure effort has to a think tank, and the useful way to read it is by its bibliography rather than its press. Its publication series is numbered, dated and authored, and the sequence tells you what the organisation actually decided to work on.

The first tranche in 2024 was scoping work. Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, US Navy retired, published 'Beneath the Surface: We May Learn More about UAP by Looking in the Ocean' in March 2024, arguing that transmedium UAP and unidentified submersible objects are the overlooked domain. The foundation itself published 'UAP in Crowded Skies' in the same month and 'Anomalous Health Threats: Health Security Considerations for UAP' in July. Helen McCaw wrote on UK policy implications in May 2024. Paul Thigpen wrote on the Catholic Church's likely response in July.

Then in November 2024 Peter Skafish, the foundation's executive director and co-founder, published 'The Prospect of Executive Branch Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Secrecy: The Harm to Congress and Potential Remedies.' That is the pivot. The subject is no longer whether UAP are anomalous; it is whether an executive-branch secrecy regime has concealed decades of knowledge, research programmes and potentially retrieved technologies from Congress itself, and what remedies exist. In May 2025 the series published Colonel Karl E. Nell, retired, on 'The UFO Enigma: A Call for US Government Disclosure,' framed around the effort to revive the UAP Disclosure Act.

By 2026 the series had become numbered Science and Policy Briefings and had acquired an author who is the point of the whole exercise. Briefing No. 2, April 2026, is 'UAP Whistleblower Restitution Fund Would Incentivize Disclosures to Congress,' by Kirk McConnell. McConnell is Sol's senior legislative affairs officer and spent thirty-seven years on the staff of the Senate Armed Services Committee. The paper's premise is stated at the top: individuals considering disclosing information about classified executive-branch UAP programmes to Congress face significant retaliation, including immediate loss of livelihood, and several senators have said so. That is a former SASC staffer, writing under a foundation's imprint, designing a financial instrument to overcome the deterrent that the special-access architecture places on contractor and government employees who might talk to his old committee. Briefings No. 1 and No. 3, both by Marik von Rennenkampff, cover Beatriz Villarroel's pre-satellite-era night-sky plate work and what the foundation describes as the first peer-reviewed scientific analysis of possible UAP materials, involving Garry Nolan.

That combination - a Stanford immunologist doing materials and biological analysis, a career Senate Armed Services staffer doing legislative design, an anthropologist doing the theory of secrecy - is why Sol belongs on this map. It is not a UFO group. It is a small institution that identified the mechanism, staffed against it, and published a remedy.

The consequence arrived in June 2026. Following what the foundation calls 'historic releases of government records about UAP encounters,' the Office of the Director of National Intelligence, with the support of the FBI, announced a UAP Science Advisory Council led by Avi Loeb, who sits on Sol's own advisory board. The foundation's announcement states that among the fourteen scientists and experts named were Sol co-founders Garry Nolan and Peter Skafish; Sol senior adviser Tim Gallaudet; and Sol advisory board member Jacob Haqq-Misra. Ross Howard, who leads production for the foundation, would lead communications for the council. The council reports to a higher-level UAP Governance Board, 'an interagency body coordinating across military, law enforcement, and intelligence community agencies,' and 'All data shared with the council will be unclassified.' The foundation's own framing is unambiguous about what it thinks happened: 'The appointment of Sol's co-founders, Admiral (ret.) Gallaudet and Dr. Jacob Haqq-Misra to the government's scientific advisory body is a fulfillment of its founding mission. Sol can now share its insight to the highest levels of government deliberation about disclosure, amplifying our impact.'

Two things about that are worth holding separately. The first is a genuine institutional achievement: a private foundation went from formation to supplying a plurality of the government's outside scientific advisers on this subject in under three years. The second is a caution the board should apply without softening it: the council those people joined is, on the government's own description, unclassified-only. A body with no access to classified material cannot examine recovered material, cannot audit a carve-out special access programme, and cannot compel anything. Sol's own executive director wrote the paper explaining exactly why that matters. The organisation that best articulated the executive-branch secrecy problem has now placed its people inside a body that has been constituted on the far side of the secrecy line.

The last thing to say about Sol is the thing it does not say about itself. Its site solicits membership and donations. It names no donors, no foundations and no corporate supporters. For an entity on a board whose organising question is who pays, that is the largest single documentary gap in its record, and it is not resolved by anything published as of this writing. There is also a small unresolved inconsistency in its own account of its founding date - the June 2026 announcement says the foundation 'was established in 2023,' which does not match every version the organisation has published.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Who funds the Sol Foundation. No donor, foundation or corporate supporter is named in any published Sol material, and there is no public filing reviewed here that discloses its revenue sources.
- The foundation's own founding date is inconsistent across its publications (2022 in some material, 'established in 2023' in the June 2026 council announcement); the incorporation record has not been checked.
- Whether any Sol principal or adviser holds or has held a security clearance or special-access indoctrination that would constrain what they can say about the concealment thesis they publish on.
- Whether the McConnell whistleblower restitution fund proposal has been introduced in any legislative vehicle, and by whom.
- What relationship, if any, exists between Sol and the government bodies its people now advise beyond the individual appointments - whether any contract, agreement, data-sharing arrangement or funding flows between the foundation and ODNI, AARO or the FBI.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 63 of 1138, score 75). 20 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D3.

## Immaculate Constellation disclosure, October-November 2024

Immaculate Constellation disclosure, October-November 2024 is a event on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024-10-18; axes C2 A3 D3; 18 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Alleged unacknowledged SAP; 12-page report delivered by Shellenberger and entered into the Congressional Record 13 Nov 2024.

### What it is

Immaculate Constellation is the name of an alleged unacknowledged special access program that entered public reporting in October 2024 through the journalist Michael Shellenberger and entered the Congressional Record on 13 November 2024. According to the whistleblower report Shellenberger delivered to the House Oversight Committee, it was created by the Department of Defense in 2017 after the New York Times published on AATIP, and functions as a central or parent USAP consolidating UAP observations from tasked and untasked collection platforms. The Department of Defense has stated it has no record of any such program.

### Why it is implicated

The allegation is not that a secret exists but that a specific legal mechanism was used to keep it from Congress. Shellenberger's sworn written testimony states that a source told him the SAP 'is controlled by the White House and executed and administrated by the DOD to avoid compliance with Title 10 of the United States Code', adding: 'It is essentially a DOD Program, as the White House directed the SECDEF to run it. But that's the loophole.' The same testimony identifies the statutes that should have compelled notification - 10 U.S.C. 119 and 50 U.S.C. 3093 - and asserts that the White House has a loophole for non-covert-action waived programs. That is a falsifiable structural claim about how a compartment can be run without triggering either SAP notification to the congressional defense committees or covert action findings to the intelligence committees. If true, it explains the entire pattern of this board: programmes that no oversight body can locate because no oversight body was ever the notification recipient.

### The longer account

In October 2024 Michael Shellenberger, founder of the outlet Public, reported that a current or former US government official acting as a UAP whistleblower had provided him a report naming an active and highly secretive Department of Defense unacknowledged special access program. On 13 November 2024 he appeared under oath before the House Oversight Committee and the twelve-page document was entered into the Congressional Record from the dais.

His written testimony sets out the constitutional frame before it names anything. It cites 10 U.S.C. 119 and 50 U.S.C. 3093 - the SAP notification statute and the covert action statute - and states: 'DEPSECDEF must notify congress regarding DOD SAPs and the President must notify regarding Covert Action. For UAPs this is apparently not happening because the White House has a loophole for non-covert action waived programs.' The whistleblower's own words, as quoted in the testimony, are that 'the Executive Branch has been managing UAP/NHI issues without Congressional knowledge, oversight, or authorization for some time, quite possibly decades.'

The source characterisation of the programme is that it 'is a "strategic intelligence program" that is part of the US military's family of long-standing, highly-sensitive programs dealing with various aspects of the UAP "problem."' The report itself, as quoted in the testimony, says 'IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION serves as a central or "parent" USAP that consolidates observations' of UAPs 'by both tasked and untasked collection platforms.' It 'includes high-quality Imagery Intelligence (IMINT) and Measurement and Signature Intelligence (MASINT) of UAPs', drawn from 'a blend of directed and incidental collection capabilities positioned in Low Earth Orbit (LEO), the upper atmosphere' and at aviation and maritime altitudes. The most common shapes in the dataset are given as 'spheres/orbs, discs/saucers, ovals/tic-tacs, triangles, boomerang/arrowhead, and irregular/organic.'

The incident material is specific enough to be checkable by anyone with access to the underlying databases. One case: orbs surrounding and forcing an F-22 out of its patrol area, in which 'The F-22 broke trajectory and attempted to evade but was intercepted and boxed in by approximately 3-6 UAPs', with one UAP maintaining 'a rigid spatial relationship with the F-22, maintaining its exact position and orientation parallel with the F-22's cockpit despite multiple evasive rolls and maneuvers.' Another: the crew of a Navy aircraft carrier watching a 'small orange-red sphere' rapidly descend from high altitude to 100-200 yards directly above the flight deck. A third, sourced after publication: a roughly thirteen-minute high-definition full-colour video of a white orb emerging from the ocean approximately twenty miles off the coast of Kuwait, filmed from a helicopter, joined mid-video by a second orb, discovered by the source on SIPR.

The testimony also records a warning about the name itself. 'A source warned that simply printing the name "Immaculate Constellation" could trigger government surveillance of me under the Foreign Intelligence Surveillance Act (FISA) of whoever publishes it. "They won't comment on it, but talking about it will put you in the danger zone," I was told.' From the dais that day Rep. Nancy Mace said the same thing out loud: 'And Representative Luna just told me, if I say, "Immaculate Constellation," I will be on some list. Maybe a FISA warrant. So, come at me bro, I guess.'

The Department's response is a records assertion, and Shellenberger printed it in his own testimony: 'DOD spokesperson Sue Gough said, "The DOD has no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called 'IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION.'"' That is not a statement that the programme does not exist. It is a statement about what a records search returned, made by a public affairs office that would not hold the read-on to an unacknowledged compartment. Mace's framing on the dais captured the structure exactly: '12 pages about this unacknowledged special access program that your government says does not exist.'

Two further items from the same hearing bear on the plausibility of the mechanism rather than the name. Retired Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet, formerly the Navy's chief meteorologist, testified: 'I concluded that the UAP information must have been classified within a special access program managed by an intelligence agency. That is, a compartmented program that even senior officials, including myself, were not read into.' And Luis Elizondo, asked directly by Mace whether he was read into secret UAP crash retrieval programmes, answered: 'We would have to have a conversation in a closed session, ma'am. I signed documentation 3 years ago that restricts my ability to discuss specifically crash retrievals.'

Under questioning from Rep. Clay Higgins, Shellenberger confirmed he knows the report's author - 'I do' - and declined to characterise the author's institutional position. Asked by Mace what the programme's mission is, he answered that it is an unacknowledged special access program whose 'mission is to document UAPs'. He also stated that when he checked the report against existing public cases he 'did not find it based on existing cases; it was new cases for me', which he offered as evidence against circular reporting.

What the record does not contain is any independent corroboration of the name from a second identified source, any document bearing the programme name, or any executive branch statement beyond the records denial. The name has never appeared in an AARO annual report, in any PURSUE tranche description, or in any published DoD product.

### What it has denied

Department of Defense spokesperson Sue Gough is quoted in Shellenberger's written testimony as saying: 'The DOD has no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called "IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION."' That is a records assertion rather than a denial of existence, and it is the standard formulation available to an office without read-on to an unacknowledged compartment. Rep. Mace characterised it from the dais as a programme 'that your government says does not exist'. No further departmental statement has been made in the twenty-one months since.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The identity and institutional position of the report's author, which Shellenberger confirmed he knows but declined to disclose.
- Whether any congressional committee has formally requested a SAP notification search under 10 U.S.C. 119 against the alleged programme, and what the response was.
- Whether the 'non-covert action waived program' loophole described in the testimony is a real category in current SAP practice, and which office administers such programmes.
- Whether the specific incidents described - the F-22 boxing event, the CVN flight deck sphere, the Kuwait helicopter video on SIPR - correspond to identifiable case numbers in AARO, UAPTF or service databases.
- No document bearing the programme name has ever been published; the only artefact in the public record is the twelve-page report entered by Rep. Mace.
- Claims elsewhere that former AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick denied the programme could not be verified against a primary source for this dossier and are not asserted here.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 25 of 1138, score 100). 19 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 6 official denials recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

## House Oversight joint hearing, 13 November 2024 - 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth'

House Oversight joint hearing, 13 November 2024 - 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth' is a event on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024-11-13; axes C2 A2 D3; 16 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: congressional-hearings.

### What it is

A joint hearing of the House Committee on Oversight and Accountability's Subcommittee on Cybersecurity, Information Technology, and Government Innovation and its Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, titled 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth,' held 13 November 2024 at 11:30 a.m. in 2154 Rayburn House Office Building. The four witnesses were Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet USN (Ret.), Luis Elizondo, Michael Gold, and Michael Shellenberger. Written testimony from all four is published on the committee's website.

### Why it is implicated

This is the hearing that entered the name of an alleged active Unacknowledged Special Access Program, IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION, into the congressional record, together with the sourced allegation that the program is controlled by the White House and executed by DoD specifically 'to avoid compliance with Title 10 of the United States Code.' It is also the hearing at which a retired flag officer testified under his own name that a UAP safety-of-flight email was deleted from Navy accounts overnight, and at which a former DoD official who managed a Special Access Program for the White House and NSC testified that 'the U.S. is in possession of UAP technologies.' The same witness testified that the Pentagon's Public Affairs Office employs a psychological operations officer as the single point of contact for UAP inquiries, and another witness described being subjected to an hours-long influence operation by the then-acting AARO director. The hearing converts whistleblower assertions into dated, attributable, congressionally-published documents.

### The longer account

The November 2024 hearing was the third in the House Oversight sequence that began with the July 2023 Grusch hearing, and it is the one that matters most structurally, because it moved the argument from 'there are unexplained objects' to 'there is a named program and a named legal evasion.'

Michael Shellenberger's written testimony carries the load. He reported that a current or former US government official acting as a whistleblower had written a report, provided to authorised committees of Congress and their staff, stating that 'the Executive Branch has been managing UAP/NHI issues without Congressional knowledge, oversight, or authorization for some time, quite possibly decades.' The report names an active DoD Unacknowledged Special Access Program, IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION, described by the document's source as a 'strategic intelligence program' that is part of the military's family of long-standing highly-sensitive programs dealing with aspects of the UAP problem. The report states that 'IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION serves as a central or parent USAP that consolidates observations' of UAP 'by both tasked and untasked collection platforms,' and that it 'includes high-quality Imagery Intelligence (IMINT) and Measurement and Signature Intelligence (MASINT) of UAPs,' sourced from a blend of directed and incidental collection in Low Earth Orbit, the upper atmosphere, aviation altitudes and maritime environments. The whistleblower alleges the USAP was created in 2017, after the New York Times published its AATIP story.

The program's stated function, as quoted in the testimony, is the concealment mechanism itself: 'the existence of IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION demonstrates the extant capability to detect, quarantine, and transfer UAP and RV [Reproduction Vehicle] collection incidents before they are observed and circulated within the Military Intelligence Enterprise, thus serving as a means of enforcing internal information security.' That is a claim about a filter operating upstream of the intelligence enterprise, and it is the single most consequential sentence in the hearing record.

The legal mechanism follows. DoD spokesperson Sue Gough issued a categorical denial: 'The DOD has no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION.' After publication, Shellenberger says a source told him the SAP 'is controlled by the White House and executed and administrated by the DOD to avoid compliance with Title 10 of the United States Code', 'It is essentially a DOD Program, as the White House directed the SECDEF to run it. But that's the loophole.' A former IC official told him: 'That program is run out of SEC DEF. They don't want to acknowledge it's real.' Shellenberger also notes that AARO is housed within OUSD(I&S), so 'the office that Congress created to investigate unreported UAP programs is troublingly close to the organizations running the alleged programs.' A source warned him that merely printing the program name could trigger FISA surveillance of whoever published it.

The testimony also describes two incidents from the whistleblower's report. An F-22 'broke trajectory and attempted to evade but was intercepted and boxed in by approximately 3-6 UAPs,' with one holding station within twelve metres starboard of the cockpit, 'maintaining its exact position and orientation parallel with the F-22's cockpit despite multiple evasive rolls and maneuvers,' the aircraft then 'forced out of the mission area under the escort of the UAP formation.' Separately, an aircraft carrier's crew observed a 'small orange-red sphere' descend to 100-200 yards above the flight deck, emitting light that 'did not illuminate the ocean or the flight deck of the CVN despite the visual appearance of intense luminosity,' with a surface 'roiling like the surface of the sun.'

Tim Gallaudet's testimony supplies the institutional-deletion evidence. In January 2015, as Commander of Naval Meteorology and Oceanography Command, he received an email on the Navy's secure network from the Fleet Forces Command operations officer, addressed to all subordinate commanders, subject line in capitals: 'URGENT SAFETY OF FLIGHT ISSUE,' attaching what is now the 'Go Fast' video. 'The very next day, the email disappeared from my account and those of the other recipients without explanation,' and neither the Commander of Fleet Forces nor his operations officer ever raised it again. Gallaudet's inference is stated plainly: 'I concluded that the UAP information must have been classified within a special access program managed by an intelligence agency.' He further testified that in 2024 he was the object of an hours-long influence operation by the then-acting AARO director and senior staff, aimed at validating AARO's historical records report, questioning the Nimitz tic tac case, and disparaging former officials who have spoken publicly. He told the committee that on 11 November 2024, two days before the hearing, Chris Mellon had asked for his help getting Congress details of a 2017 case involving satellite imagery of UAP 'that has still not been shared with Congress.'

Luis Elizondo's testimony is the shortest and the most direct: 'UAP are real. Advanced technologies not made by our Government, or any other government, are monitoring sensitive military installations around the globe. Furthermore, the U.S. is in possession of UAP technologies, as are some of our adversaries. I believe we are in the midst of a multi-decade, secretive arms race, one funded by misallocated taxpayer dollars and hidden from our elected representatives and oversight bodies.' He established his standing in the same paragraph: 'In my last position, I managed a Special Access Program on behalf of the White House and the National Security Council.' He described a 'small cadre' inside government that has created 'a culture of suppression and intimidation,' including 'unwarranted criminal investigations, harassment, and efforts to destroy one's credibility,' and stated that many of his former colleagues have given classified testimony to the DoD and Intelligence Community Inspectors General and were subsequently targeted with threats to their careers, clearances 'and even our lives.'

Michael Gold's testimony is the counterweight in tone but not in direction: a former NASA associate administrator arguing that stigma, not evidence, is the binding constraint.

What the record does not settle: no committee has published the underlying Immaculate Constellation report, no dates or units are attached to the F-22 or carrier incidents, and the acting AARO director in Gallaudet's account is not named in his written testimony.

### What it has denied

DoD spokesperson Sue Gough issued a categorical, attributable denial quoted into the hearing record: 'The DOD has no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION.' The denial is framed as a records statement rather than a factual one, and is exactly the form of denial the alleged White-House-controlled, DoD-executed structure would produce.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Who is the IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION whistleblower, and did any committee formally obtain and retain the underlying report?
- What dates, units and locations attach to the F-22 encounter and the carrier flight-deck sphere described in the report?
- Who was the acting AARO director present at the meeting Gallaudet describes as an influence operation, and who were the senior staff?
- Was Chris Mellon's 2017 satellite-imagery case ever briefed to the oversight committees, and which agency holds the imagery?
- Did DoD's denial address only the exact string 'IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION,' and would a nickname change or an unacknowledged compartment render the denial technically accurate?
- Is there a written record of the Fleet Forces Command email Gallaudet describes, and was its deletion a routine recall or a directed action?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 42 of 1138, score 87). 18 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A2/D3.

## ODNI Preliminary Assessment: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (25 June 2021)

ODNI Preliminary Assessment: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (25 June 2021) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2021; axes C0 A2 D3; 13 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: report.

### What it is

A nine-page unclassified intelligence product titled 'Preliminary Assessment: Unidentified Aerial Phenomena,' issued by the Office of the Director of National Intelligence on 25 June 2021 in response to a provision in Senate Report 116-233 accompanying the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2021. It was drafted by the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force and the ODNI National Intelligence Manager for Aviation, with input from sixteen named agencies and components, and prepared for the Congressional Intelligence and Armed Services Committees. It reviewed 144 U.S. Government UAP reports from November 2004 to March 2021 and resolved exactly one.

### Why it is implicated

This is the document in which the U.S. Intelligence Community, in writing and on the record, declined to attribute a single one of 143 unresolved incidents to American classified programs, and admitted it could not confirm that its own developmental programs accounted for any of them. That admission cuts both ways and the board should hold both edges. If the objects are not U.S. black programs, the IC has just conceded 143 unexplained penetrations of restricted airspace, many near nuclear-capable and test infrastructure. If they are U.S. black programs, then the DNI's own coordinating body was denied read-in access to them, which is the definition of the concealment the board asserts. The assessment further records that UAP cluster around U.S. training and testing grounds and near facilities carrying the most advanced sensor systems, that in eighteen incidents observers reported unusual flight characteristics, that military aircraft systems processed radio-frequency energy associated with sightings, and that the task force holds data appearing to show acceleration or signature management. It is also a concealment artifact in its own right: it names the accountable official only by title, ships the substance to a classified annex, and defines the problem as a data-collection deficiency rather than an access deficiency, converting a question about who is hiding what into a request for better forms and more money.

### The longer account

The assessment exists because of a paragraph in a Senate committee report, not because of an executive decision to inform the public. Senate Report 116-233, submitted by Senator Marco Rubio from the Select Committee on Intelligence on 17 June 2020 to accompany S. 3905, directed the DNI, in consultation with the Secretary of Defense, to submit an intelligence assessment of the threat posed by UAP and of the progress the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force had made. Appendix B of the resulting product reprints the eight specific things the Senate asked for. Two of them are worth memorizing. Item 4 demanded 'a detailed description of an interagency process for ensuring timely data collection and centralized analysis of all UAP reporting for the Federal Government, regardless of which service or agency acquired the information.' Item 5 demanded 'Identification of an official accountable for the process described in paragraph 4.' The Senate was asking, in oversight language, who owns the file. ODNI's answer, in the Scope section, is a title with no name attached: 'The Director, UAPTF, is the accountable official for ensuring the timely collection and consolidation of data on UAP.'

The drafting coalition is itself a finding. The report was written by the UAPTF and the ODNI National Intelligence Manager for Aviation, with input from USD(I&S), DIA, FBI, NRO, NGA, NSA, Air Force, Army, Navy, Navy/ONI, DARPA, FAA, NOAA, ODNI/NIM-Emerging and Disruptive Technology, ODNI's National Counterintelligence and Security Center, and the National Intelligence Council. The Department of Energy is not on the list. Neither is CIA. Neither is any Special Access Program Central Office. For a document whose central unresolved question is whether the objects are American, the absence of the organizations that would custody the most sensitive American aerospace and nuclear-adjacent programs is the shape of the hole.

The numbers are small and precise. One hundred forty-four reports originated from USG sources. Eighty of those involved observation with multiple sensors: radar, infrared, electro-optical, weapon seekers, visual. One was identified with high confidence, 'a large, deflating balloon.' The rest, 143, 'remain unexplained.' In eighteen incidents, described across twenty-one reports, observers reported unusual movement or flight characteristics: objects that appeared to remain stationary in winds aloft, move against the wind, maneuver abruptly, or travel at considerable speed 'without discernable means of propulsion.' In a small number of cases 'military aircraft systems processed radio frequency (RF) energy associated with UAP sightings.' The UAPTF 'holds a small amount of data that appear to show UAP demonstrating acceleration or a degree of signature management,' with further analysis promised. Eleven documented near misses were on file.

The five-bin taxonomy, airborne clutter, natural atmospheric phenomena, USG or industry developmental programs, foreign adversary systems, and a catchall 'other,' is where the board plants its flag. Under the third bin the report says: 'Some UAP observations could be attributable to developments and classified programs by U.S. entities. We were unable to confirm, however, that these systems accounted for any of the UAP reports we collected.' That is not a denial that such programs exist. It is a statement that the drafters could not obtain confirmation. An intelligence assessment written by the DNI's aviation manager and coordinated across sixteen organizations was unable to check its own government's classified aerospace inventory against its own government's unexplained-object file. Either no such inventory exists, or access to it was refused, or the query was never seriously pressed. Each possibility is load-bearing.

The geographic finding is handled the same way. UAP sightings 'tended to cluster around U.S. training and testing grounds,' and 'some UAP have been detected near military facilities or by aircraft carrying the USG's most advanced sensor systems.' The report immediately neutralizes this as probable collection bias: more sensors, greater expectation, standing guidance to report anomalies. That may be true. It is also exactly the explanation one would offer to avoid the alternative reading, which is that the objects are interested in specific American infrastructure, the same pattern documented at Loring, Wurtsmith, Malmstrom, Minot and Falconbridge in October and November 1975.

The remedies proposed are all inputs, never access. Standardize reporting. Consolidate data. Employ artificial intelligence and machine learning to cluster features. Search historical radar archives with advanced algorithms. Update the interagency collection strategy. Increase R&D investment guided by a UAP Collection Strategy, a UAP R&D Technical Roadmap, and a UAP Program Plan. Not one recommendation asks any agency to disclose what it already holds. The report also records, candidly, that no standardized reporting mechanism existed anywhere in the U.S. military until the Navy created one in March 2019 and the Air Force adopted it in November 2020, meaning that for the entire post-Blue Book era the United States operated with no formal channel by which a military aviator could report an object over a nuclear or test facility. That is not an accident of bureaucracy; it is a design decision with a fifty-year half-life. The report is equally candid about the human cost: aviators and analysts 'describe disparagement associated with observing UAP, reporting it, or attempting to discuss it with colleagues.'

The institutional sequel followed within six months. Section 1683 of the FY2022 National Defense Authorization Act, Public Law 117-81, signed 27 December 2021, directed establishment of a successor office. The Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group was stood up and then superseded; the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office was formally established by a Deputy Secretary of Defense memorandum published 20 July 2022, designating AARO the authoritative DoD focal point for UAP and placing it under USD(I&S), one of the very components that had contributed to this assessment. The task force that could not confirm whether American programs accounted for any of its own 144 cases was thereby replaced by an office that would go on to assert that no such programs ever existed. The 2021 assessment is the last moment the U.S. government's written position was uncertainty rather than denial, which is precisely why it is the most useful single document in the modern record.

### What it has denied

The assessment does not deny that a U.S. legacy program exists; it declines to confirm one, in the specific formulation 'We were unable to confirm, however, that these systems accounted for any of the UAP reports we collected.' That is a sourcing statement about the drafters' own access, not a factual finding about the U.S. inventory, and it is signed institutionally by ODNI rather than by any named individual.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Who was the Director, UAPTF, on 25 June 2021, and why is that individual unnamed in a document responding to a Senate demand to name the accountable official?
- What is in the classified annex, and which committees received it?
- By what procedure did the UAPTF attempt to check the 144 reports against U.S. classified developmental programs, which offices were queried, and which declined?
- Why are the Department of Energy and CIA absent from the contributor list on a report whose central unresolved bin is 'USG or industry developmental programs'?
- What became of the promised 'further analysis' of the acceleration and signature-management data, and does a completed product exist?
- Do FAA or NOAA holdings referenced in the report contain UAP data predating November 2004 that was excluded from the dataset by scoping rather than by absence?
- Which of the 144 cases, if any, occurred over or near nuclear weapons storage, ICBM fields or nuclear-powered vessels, and was that variable ever coded?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 105 of 1138, score 62). 14 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A2/D3.

## Immaculate Constellation 12-page report entered into the Congressional Record

Immaculate Constellation 12-page report entered into the Congressional Record is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024; axes C2 A3 D3; 12 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Whistleblower report, entered 13 Nov 2024.

### What it is

A twelve-page document that Michael Shellenberger delivered to Congress on the morning of 13 November 2024 and that Rep. Nancy Mace moved into the Congressional Record from the dais during the House Oversight Committee hearing 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth'. Its first section describes an unacknowledged special access program named Immaculate Constellation; its second section concerns United States Government imagery intelligence. It is listed in the hearing's Index of Documents as 'Report, Pentagon, "Immaculate Constellation"; submitted by Rep. Mace.'

### Why it is implicated

This is the only artefact in the entire public record that purports to be an internal description of a UAP parent compartment, and a chairwoman of a House committee put it into the Congressional Record while the Department of Defense was on record saying it has no record of the programme it names. The document is therefore a test case for the whole disclosure machinery: a congressional committee can receive, distribute to every member on the dais, and formally enter a document about an unacknowledged SAP, and nothing follows. No agency was compelled to search for it, no author was subpoenaed, no classification review was triggered, and no executive branch office has addressed its contents. It is the disclosure mechanism working exactly as designed and producing exactly nothing.

### The longer account

The document arrived on the morning of the hearing. Rep. Mace flagged it in her opening remarks before the witnesses were sworn: 'there is a document that will be entered into the congressional record today. Mr. Tim Burchett from Tennessee has this document, and we just distributed it to every Member up here on the dais, this document. But this is going to be the original document from the Pentagon about Immaculate Constellation that Michael Shellenberger delivered to Congress today.' She then characterised it: '12 pages about this unacknowledged special access program that your government says does not exist.'

Later in the hearing, after the last of the opening statements, she performed the formal act: 'Committee staff asked me to go ahead, and I will do it, to enter into the Congressional Record this 12-page document that Michael Shellenberger brought today that describes the Immaculate Constellation government program. So, we will do that now. Every Member up here has a copy of it.' She then described its structure on the record: 'The first section talks about the unacknowledged special access program called Immaculate Constellation, and the second section about USG imagery intelligence.' She added the FISA warning she had just received from Rep. Luna, and closed with 'But, without objection, entered into the record.'

The printed hearing record's Index of Documents lists it as 'Report, Pentagon, "Immaculate Constellation"; submitted by Rep. Mace', alongside a Mellon hearing letter submitted by Mace, documents related to UAPs submitted by Burchett, and Questions for the Record to Elizondo, Gallaudet, Shellenberger and Gold submitted by Burlison. The record states that 'The documents listed above are available at: docs.house.gov.'

The contents, as read into the record by Shellenberger in his oral statement and set out at greater length in his written testimony, are consistent between the two. The report describes a parent USAP consolidating UAP observations from tasked and untasked collection platforms; a holdings profile of high-quality IMINT and MASINT from low Earth orbit, the upper atmosphere, aviation altitudes and maritime environments; a shape taxonomy of spheres and orbs, discs and saucers, ovals and tic-tacs, triangles, boomerang and arrowhead forms and irregular or organic shapes; and seven categories of evidence. The written testimony says the report 'describes in detail various UAP sightings collected by technical assets and US military personnel', and that the underlying material sits in defence HUMINT databases 'accessible to the Intelligence Community'.

Two incident descriptions are quoted directly from the report in the written testimony. The F-22 encounter: 'The F-22 broke trajectory and attempted to evade but was intercepted and boxed in by approximately 3-6 UAPs', with one object holding station off the cockpit through multiple evasive manoeuvres before the aircraft 'was forced out of the mission area under the escort of the UAP formation.' And the carrier deck event, in which a small orange-red sphere descended from high altitude to within 100-200 yards directly above the flight deck of a CVN.

The evidentiary character of the document is worth stating plainly, because the argument does not need it inflated. It is an anonymous report, delivered by a journalist, whose author Shellenberger says he knows and will not name. Under questioning from Rep. Higgins he confirmed 'I do' when asked whether he knew the author, and when pressed on the author's sources referred back to the report itself. He offered one internal check: that when he compared its cases against publicly known cases he 'did not find it based on existing cases; it was new cases for me', which he presented as an argument against circular reporting. That is a weak check, and he said so in effect by presenting it as the only one he could make.

What gives the document its standing is not its provenance but its position. It sits in the Congressional Record. It was distributed to every member on the dais of a House committee. It names a compartment. And the executive branch response to it, before and since, has been a single sentence from a public affairs office - Sue Gough's statement that 'The DOD has no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called "IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION"' - which addresses records holdings and not existence, and which was itself printed in the witness's own testimony rather than issued in response to the congressional entry.

Since 13 November 2024 the document has been followed by nothing. No agency has confirmed conducting a search. No committee has published a referral. No classification authority has asserted that the document is classified, which would be the ordinary response to an authentic Pentagon report on an unacknowledged SAP appearing in a public hearing room; nor has any authority asserted that it is a forgery, which would be the ordinary response to a fabrication. That double silence is the most interesting fact about the artefact. The disclosure machinery accepted the document and then had nowhere to put it, because the mechanism Congress built in 2023 - a records collection administered by the agencies that hold the records - has no procedure for a document that arrives from outside the agencies and describes a compartment the agencies say they cannot find.

### What it has denied

The Department of Defense has said only that it has 'no record, present or historical, of any type of SAP called "IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION"', a statement quoted in the witness's own written testimony rather than issued in response to the document's entry into the Congressional Record. No agency has asserted that the twelve-page document is classified, and no agency has asserted that it is a forgery.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the twelve-page document as entered by Rep. Mace is in fact retrievable at docs.house.gov, as the Index of Documents states, and in what form.
- Whether the document carries any classification markings, originator identifiers, or control numbers, none of which have been described publicly.
- Whether any agency conducted a formal records search in response to the congressional entry, as distinct from the earlier press-office response.
- The full text of the report's second section on US Government imagery intelligence has never been publicly summarised in detail.
- Whether the Questions for the Record submitted to Shellenberger by Rep. Burlison addressed the report's provenance, and whether the answers were published.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 26 of 1138, score 99). 14 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 4 official denials recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

## ODNI Memorandum ES 2026-00818 - Preliminary Guidance for Implementation of NDA Waiver

ODNI Memorandum ES 2026-00818 - Preliminary Guidance for Implementation of NDA Waiver is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2026-08-01; axes C2 A3 D3; 10 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Signed by Aaron Lukas, Principal Deputy DNI; released by Rep. Burlison 1 August 2026.

### What it is

An Office of the Director of National Intelligence memorandum titled "Preliminary Guidance for Implementation of NDA Waiver," bearing control number ES 2026-00818, dated 31 July 2026, from the Principal Deputy Director of National Intelligence to the Director of the Central Intelligence Agency and other IC element heads. It directs the Department of War and the Intelligence Community to build a process by which current and former employees and contractors may disclose UAP information to AARO or to designated representatives of the Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters (PURSUE) Task Force notwithstanding existing non-disclosure agreements, agreements, oaths or commitments.

### Why it is implicated

The memo is an admission written in the form of a remedy. No government waives non-disclosure obligations for a subject about which nothing is being withheld. Its most consequential sentence is the one instructing the IC to make its workforce aware that any NDA prohibiting disclosure to the President, the PURSUE Task Force or other designees is no longer in force, an instruction that only makes sense if such instruments exist, and DLA Piper's government contracts practice notes that an NDA barring disclosure to the President "would be unusual if not unlawful" because the President is the ultimate classification authority. It was not published by ODNI. The administration authorised the House office of Rep. Eric Burlison, a founding member of the Congressional UAP Caucus, to distribute it, routing an IC-wide directive through the oversight bloc rather than dni.gov. And it is materially narrower than the statute it purports to implement: 50 U.S.C. § 3373b already authorises disclosure to AARO notwithstanding Executive Order 13526, the Atomic Energy Act, and Section 798 of the Espionage Act, while the memo reaches only contractual and oath-based restrictions.

### The longer account

On 31 July 2026 the Principal Deputy Director of National Intelligence signed a memorandum, numbered ES 2026-00818, addressed to the Director of the CIA and the heads of the other Intelligence Community elements. Two days later Rep. Eric Burlison (MO-07) posted it on his House website, noting that "the administration authorized Congressman Burlison's office to publicly distribute the memorandum." That single procedural fact deserves more attention than the memo's contents. The executive branch chose, as the public release channel for a national-level intelligence directive, the congressional office of a member who had spent three years accusing the executive branch of hiding UAP records, and who ten days earlier, on 21 July 2026, had publicly invited anyone who believed the President's directive applied to them to contact his office for help navigating the process. Burlison's office was, in effect, standing up a parallel intake channel for whistleblowers while the official one was still being written.

What the memo does: it directs the Department of War and the IC to implement a process ensuring that current and former governmental employees and contractors holding alleged UAP information may disclose it to designated government representatives in AARO or the PURSUE Task Force notwithstanding contravening NDAs, agreements, oaths or commitments. It instructs every IC element, within thirty days of receipt, to coordinate with ODNI to establish a PURSUE designee and to "inform their workforce of the President's order." It states that NDAs prohibiting disclosure to the President, to PURSUE or to other designees are no longer in force. And it flags two further tranches of guidance to come, implementing guidance for the preliminary guidance itself, and a separate ODNI/DoW package for the broader PURSUE transparency project.

What the memo does not do is the more informative half. It does not authorise public disclosure of classified information. It does not authorise disclosure to Congress other than through AARO or existing channels, a striking limitation given that the standard ODNI classified information nondisclosure agreement, Standard Form 312, already contemplates disclosure to Congress in some form. It does not tell government contractors, as distinct from IC employees, how to make an authorised disclosure at all, even though the FY2023 NDAA's anti-retaliation protection expressly extends to any "contractor, subcontractor, grantee, subgrantee, or personal services contractor" of a covered department or agency. For an entity board built on the premise that the retrieval and reverse-engineering work sits inside cleared private industry, that omission is the operative fact: the memo waives the secrecy instruments of the government workforce while leaving the contractor workforce, the population most likely to hold the material, without a documented route.

The memo is also narrower than the law. Section 1673 of the FY2023 NDAA (Pub. L. No. 117-263, 136 Stat. 2959-62, codified at 50 U.S.C. § 3373b(b)(1)) authorises disclosure to AARO notwithstanding not merely NDAs but any regulation or order issued under Executive Order 13526, anything under the Atomic Energy Act, Section 798 of the Espionage Act, and any other "provision of law relating to the disclosure of information." ES 2026-00818 speaks only to "[NDAs], agreements, oaths, or commitments." As agency guidance it is not binding law, and DLA Piper's alert notes that it "does not, by itself, confer legal rights with respect to making authorized disclosures beyond those established by statute." A person weighing whether to come forward is therefore being handed a document that sounds like permission and is legally less protective than the statute already on the books, and told that the real guidance is still being drafted. That is a structure that produces delay.

The scale problem is visible in AARO's own reporting. Its Fiscal Year 2025 Consolidated Annual Report states that between 2 June 2024 and 30 May 2025, seven individuals contacted AARO with UAP-related claims that AARO judged warranted a request for a follow-on interview. Seven. The same report asserts that AARO is legally authorised to receive all UAP-related information at any level of classification regardless of restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs. Those two statements coexisting is the concealment argument in miniature: an office with unlimited statutory reach and a single-digit annual intake.

Context matters for dating. The PURSUE Task Force was launched by the Department of War on 1 May 2026 following President Trump's 19 February 2026 Truth Social directive; the first tranche of records went public on 8 May 2026, with further tranches on 22 May, 12 June, 10 July and 7 August 2026. ES 2026-00818 arrives at the end of that sequence, nearly three months into a rolling public release, the NDA waiver follows the file dumps rather than preceding them, which means the population best able to contextualise those files was still under contractual gag while the files were being published.

One further structural note. AARO sits inside the Department of War but reports to ODNI on operational and security matters under 50 U.S.C. § 3373(b)(3)(B)(ii). ES 2026-00818 was issued by ODNI, while the FY2023 NDAA assigns the establishment of the secure disclosure mechanism to the Secretary of War acting through the head of AARO in consultation with the DNI. The guidance therefore comes from the consulted party rather than the responsible one, a jurisdictional wrinkle that any contractor's counsel will find, and that gives every recipient a colourable argument that the instrument binding them has not yet been properly issued.

### What it has denied

The memo contains no denial and is therefore more damaging than one: it is a dated, numbered, signed executive-branch instrument that presupposes the existence of a body of UAP knowledge held under enforceable secrecy obligations, including obligations that purported to bind holders against telling the President. The concealment survives in the memo's boundaries rather than its assertions, no public disclosure, no route to Congress outside AARO, and no procedure at all for contractors.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Who are the PURSUE designees established at each IC element under the 30-day requirement, and has any list been published?
- Has the promised follow-on implementing guidance been issued, and does it extend a procedure to contractor personnel?
- Does the waiver reach special access program indoctrination agreements (DD Form 2836) and proprietary contractor NDAs, or only government-issued instruments such as SF 312?
- Does it reach Restricted Data under the Atomic Energy Act, 42 U.S.C. § 2162, where presidential discretion is itself constrained?
- Which specific NDAs prohibited disclosure to the President, who drafted them, and under what authority were they executed?
- How many authorised disclosures have been made to AARO or PURSUE since 31 July 2026, and by whom?
- Why was a congressional office rather than dni.gov selected as the public release channel, and who made that decision?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 79 of 1138, score 71). 10 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 4 official denials recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

## Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets

Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2025; axes C0 A1 D3; 9 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: House Oversight task force, est. 11 Feb 2025.

### What it is

The Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets is a panel of the House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, established by Chairman James Comer on 11 February 2025 and chaired by Rep. Anna Paulina Luna (R-FL), authorized for six months under Committee Rules. Its announced portfolio spans the Kennedy, Robert Kennedy and King assassination records, UAP and USO, the Epstein files, COVID-19 origins and the 9/11 files. It has become the principal congressional venue for the legacy program question since 2025.

### Why it is implicated

This is the first congressional body to run at the concealment architecture rather than at the phenomenon. Its members subpoena-adjacent letters go to contractors and federally funded research and development centers rather than to the Pentagon press office, on the theory that the records are in private hands under federal sponsorship. Its chairwoman has stated on the record that the Department of Defense refused to read her members into the relevant special access programs, naming HASC and HAC-D as the only committees with access, which converts a suspicion about compartmentation into a documented, dated institutional fact. Its member Eric Burlison has converted that finding into legislative interrogatories to MIT Lincoln Laboratory and The MITRE Corporation demanding enumeration of SAPs, ACCMs, cover programs, hidden funding and instructions to avoid certain vocabulary. And its chairwoman has used her opening statement to attack the credibility of the former AARO director by name, making the government's own denial instrument a subject of the investigation.

### The longer account

The Task Force exists because a different theory of the problem won an internal argument. For four years the congressional approach to UAP was to demand better reports from the Executive branch. The Task Force's approach is to demand records from whoever physically holds them, and to treat classification as a claim to be adjudicated rather than an answer to be accepted.

It was created on 11 February 2025 when Chairman James Comer announced the panel and named Rep. Anna Paulina Luna to lead it, following President Trump's 23 January 2025 executive order on declassification of records concerning the assassinations of President Kennedy, Senator Robert Kennedy and Dr. Martin Luther King Jr. Comer framed it in institution-repair language: 'This secrecy has sowed distrust in our institutions.' Luna's own statement laid out a portfolio nobody expected a single panel to carry: 'We will also investigate UAPs/USOs, the Epstein client list, COVID-19 origins, and the 9/11 files.' The Task Force was authorized for six months pursuant to Committee Rules, and Comer and Luna transmitted eleven letters the same day. Its first hearing addressed the JFK files; Rep. Robert Garcia (D-CA) was initially the ranking member, and by the September 2025 UAP hearing Luna was yielding to Ranking Member Crockett.

The 9 September 2025 hearing, 'Restoring Public Trust Through UAP Transparency and Whistleblower Protection,' is the panel's substantive turn. Four witnesses testified: Air Force veteran Jeffrey Nuccetelli, Chief Alexandro Wiggins, journalist George Knapp, and Air Force veteran Dylan Borland. Luna's opening statement is the single most useful congressional document on the access problem. She described following a lead from active-duty Air Force pilot whistleblowers alleging a cover-up of UAP activity at Eglin Air Force Base, pursued with former Rep. Matt Gaetz and Rep. Tim Burchett. She said pilots decline to come forward out of fear for their flight status and careers. And then she stated the mechanism: 'In preparation for previous UAP hearings, the Committee repeatedly asked DoD to allow Members to view videos and files relating to UAP incidents. Unfortunately, DoD notified Committee staff that due to the Department's special access program (SAP) rules, only members of the House Armed Services Committee (HASC) and the Defense Subcommittee of the House Appropriations Committee (HAC-D) were allowed to be "read in" on such programs. For a non-committee Member to be allowed to view these documents and videos, individual Members must be approved by the Chairman and Ranking Member of both HASC and HAC-D.' She added that 'Independent SAP oversight has presented a consistent problem for Congress as the program's budgets are classified.'

That is 10 U.S.C. 119 operating as designed, quoted from the receiving end by a committee chairwoman. It also identifies precisely four people, the chairs and ranking members of HASC and HAC-D, as the gatekeepers for any Oversight Committee member seeking access.

Luna also went after AARO directly, calling former director Sean Kirkpatrick 'a documented liar' and questioning 'what his purpose at AARO really was if it was not to follow up on investigations and disclose his findings to Members of Congress.' She entered into the record former Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for Intelligence Chris Mellon's characterization of an AARO report as 'the most error-ridden and unsatisfactory government report I can recall reading during or after decades of government service,' noting Mellon's claims that it was the first AARO report submitted to Congress without the DNI's sign-off, that it omitted agencies with known UAP investigations, and that it omitted any discussion of efforts to hide information, including at U.S. Customs and Border Protection. A congressional task force treating the government's own investigative office as a suspect witness is a meaningful shift in posture.

The Task Force's method matured into contractor-directed process in 2026. On 7 May 2026 Burlison wrote to MIT Lincoln Laboratory about a reel-to-reel recording catalogued as 'AF-ATIC-FILM, 03/52' from the Beacon Hill Study, labeled 'flying saucer talk,' with Edward J. Ruppelt as briefer. On 22 May 2026 he transmitted ten pages of legislative interrogatories to MITRE, copied to Luna, Comer, and Information Security Oversight Office Director Michael D. Thomas. Both letters run on the same statutory theory: sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 NDAA, codified at 44 U.S.C. 2107 note, create a UAP Records Collection at NARA in Record Group 615 that encompasses government-funded records, and contractor custody does not defeat it. Burlison's framing in the accompanying release is the Task Force's thesis in one sentence: FFRDCs 'cannot become private vaults for federal records.'

The backdrop by 2026 is a Trump administration declassification push. Following a 19 February 2026 Trump directive, the Department of War began publishing declassified UAP files in May 2026 under the banner PURSUE, Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters, at war.gov/ufo. Burlison's press release places his MITRE inquiry explicitly inside that effort, testing whether agencies, contractors, FFRDCs and laboratories are actually complying.

### What it has denied

The Task Force issues no denials; it catalogues them. Its distinguishing posture is that it treats the Executive branch's own UAP investigative office as an unreliable narrator, with its chairwoman attacking the former AARO director by name on the record on 9 September 2025 and entering a former senior Pentagon intelligence official's demolition of an AARO report into the hearing record.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Was the Task Force's six-month authorization formally renewed, and by what vote or committee action? It was still operating through its members in May 2026.
- What did the 11 letters transmitted on 11 February 2025 demand, and of whom? The Committee published them as a single PDF but the recipients are not enumerated in the release text.
- Which four individuals, the chairs and ranking members of HASC and HAC-D at the relevant time, declined or failed to approve Oversight Members for UAP SAP read-in, and was any request formally denied in writing?
- What is the basis for Luna's characterization of Kirkpatrick as 'a documented liar'? Her statement asserts it without citing the documentation.
- Who are the 34 senior officials Luna referenced as having broken their silence, and is there a compiled list held by the Task Force?
- Has the Task Force ever received a classified briefing at TS/SCI/SAP level on UAP matters, and from which office?
- Did MITRE or MIT Lincoln Laboratory ever appear before the Task Force, and were transcripts taken?

## Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act - Burlison amendment to the FY2027 NDAA

Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act - Burlison amendment to the FY2027 NDAA is a legislation on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2026-07-22; axes C0 A0 D3; 9 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Adopted by the House 22-23 July 2026; pending in the Senate.

### What it is

The Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act as adopted by the U.S. House of Representatives on 22 July 2026 as an amendment to the Fiscal Year 2027 National Defense Authorization Act (H.R. 8800, 119th Congress). It would create a permanent UAP Records Collection at the National Archives, stand up an independent Senate-confirmed UAP Records Review Board with subpoena authority, and extend disclosure obligations to covered government contractors. It is the first time the full JFK-Act-style disclosure architecture has cleared a chamber of Congress since the original Schumer-Rounds text was gutted in conference in December 2023.

### Why it is implicated

The amendment is written on the assumption that UAP records exist outside the reach of ordinary congressional oversight, in agency holdings and in contractor holdings, and that the agencies holding them will not surrender them voluntarily. Three design choices give that away: the records collection is compulsory and on a statutory timeline rather than voluntary; the review board is given subpoena power rather than a request authority; and disclosure obligations are extended to 'covered government contractors,' which is meaningless unless Congress believes private firms hold federal UAP records. Burlison's own framing, that records have 'remained scattered across agencies and outside meaningful oversight,' is a sitting member of Congress stating on the record that the existing oversight architecture does not reach the material. The amendment also explicitly positions itself as statutory backstop to a presidential declassification effort (PURSUE), which is an admission that executive-branch goodwill is not considered durable. Every one of those features is a legislative inference about the concealment structure, drawn by people with access to classified briefings.

### The longer account

The Burlison amendment is the third act of a five-year legislative arc, and the arc itself is the evidence.

Act one was July 2023, when Senators Chuck Schumer and Mike Rounds introduced the UAP Disclosure Act as Senate Amendment 797 to S.2226, the FY2024 NDAA, explicitly modeled on the President John F. Kennedy Assassination Records Collection Act of 1992. That text carried a presumption of immediate disclosure, an independent review board, presidential authority to overturn board determinations, a hard 25-year outer deadline, and, the provision that made the defense industry move, federal eminent domain over 'recovered technologies of unknown origin' and biological evidence held by private persons. The Senate adopted it 84 to 0. An eminent domain clause is not written into a defense bill as an abstraction. It is written because the drafters believe there is specific property, in specific private hands, that the government cannot presently compel.

Act two was December 2023. In conference with the House, the review board, the eminent domain provision and the presidential certification mechanism were stripped. What survived became Sections 1841 through 1843 of Public Law 118-31, which directed the Archivist to establish a UAP Records Collection at NARA and required agencies to identify, organize and begin transmitting UAP records. The surviving language was the filing cabinet without the locksmith: a collection with no independent body empowered to second-guess an agency's decision to withhold, and no reach into contractor vaults. Agencies were left as the sole judges of what constituted a covered record.

Act three is 2025-2026. Burlison, a Missouri Republican on the House Oversight Committee, reintroduced the disclosure act as an NDAA amendment during FY2026 consideration; it did not reach a floor vote. He came back for FY2027. On 22 July 2026 the House adopted his amendment to H.R. 8800, and later that day passed the underlying bill 216 to 212 on Roll Call 278. The press release from his office is precise about what the amendment restores: 'a permanent UAP Records Collection at the National Archives,' 'an independent, Senate-confirmed UAP Records Review Board with subpoena authority to review records, hear testimony, and determine whether information should remain protected under standards established by law,' and extension of disclosure requirements 'to covered government contractors.' The review board is back. The contractor reach is back. Eminent domain is not mentioned.

The contractor clause is the load-bearing provision for anyone mapping the legacy program. Under the existing FY2024 architecture, a record physically held by a prime or a subcontractor under an IRAD-funded or SAP-funded effort is not obviously a 'federal record' subject to transmission. The FY2023 statutory scheme had already tried to grip this problem from a different angle: 50 U.S.C. 3373b(a)(1)(B) protects authorized disclosure of any activity 'by a department or agency of the Federal Government or a contractor of such a department or agency' relating to UAP including 'material retrieval, material analysis, reverse engineering,' and 3373b(b)(3)(A) ordered federal agencies and their contractors to conduct comprehensive searches of records relating to UAP nondisclosure orders and hand copies to AARO. A subpoena-armed review board with contractor jurisdiction is the enforcement mechanism those provisions never had.

The PURSUE relationship is the other tell. The press release says the amendment would 'support President Trump's Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters (PURSUE)' by 'bolstering in law authorities for carrying out disclosure.' PURSUE is a Department of War declassification and release effort standing at war.gov/ufo, running on rolling tranches. A legislator who trusted an executive declassification program to be complete and permanent would not spend a year of floor strategy building a Senate-confirmed board with subpoena power to sit alongside it. The amendment is insurance against the possibility that PURSUE releases what the executive branch chooses to release and stops there.

What happens next is a conference fight. The FY2027 NDAA moved to the Senate; the House-passed disclosure architecture will meet whatever the Senate produces, and the 2023 precedent is that the strongest provisions die quietly in conference without a recorded vote. Conference is where the concealment structure has historically been most effective, because it is the one stage of the legislative process with no roll call, no floor debate and no public amendment text.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- The exact amendment number and text as made in order by the House Rules Committee for H.R. 8800 has not been verified against rules.house.gov; reporting places it at amendment 1044 but this has not been confirmed against the Rules Committee record.
- Whether the amendment was adopted by voice vote, by division, or en bloc with other amendments, Roll Call 278 is the vote on passage of the underlying bill, not on the amendment itself.
- Whether the House-adopted text retains any analogue to the 2023 eminent domain provision over recovered technologies of unknown origin; the press release does not mention it.
- How the amendment defines 'covered government contractor', whether it reaches subcontractors, FFRDCs, UARCs, and university affiliates, or only prime contract holders.
- What standard of review the Board would apply, and whether the President retains override authority as in the 2023 Senate text.
- Whether the Senate companion or the FY2027 conference retains the Review Board; the 2023 precedent is removal in conference without a recorded vote.
- Whether AARO, ODNI or the Department of War took a formal position opposing the amendment, and whether any such position was transmitted to the Armed Services committees in writing.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 420 of 1138, score 34). 9 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.
