## AARO FY2024 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP

AARO FY2024 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024; axes C0 A2 D2; 8 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Published 14 November 2024; 757 new reports; 1,652 total.

### What it is

The Fiscal Year 2024 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena, produced by the Department of Defense All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office (AARO) with an information cut-off of 1 June 2024 and released 14 November 2024. It is submitted to Congress under a statutory requirement at 50 U.S.C. 3373(k) and covers UAP reports from 1 May 2023 to 1 June 2024 plus all prior-period reports not previously reported. It was drafted in coordination with more than two dozen named organisations including ODNI, the Service intelligence centres, NRO, NGA, NSA, DIA, FBI, NCIS, DoE, NASA, the NNSA and the Nuclear Regulatory Commission.

### Why it is implicated

AARO is not a neutral authority here; it is the office the Department created to be the answer to the question, and this report is the instrument by which it discharges its obligation to Congress. What makes it load-bearing is not its denials but the specific admissions embedded in them. Section IV.G is headed "AARO Possesses No Data to Indicate the Capture or Exploitation of UAP" and its entire body is a single sentence stating that AARO is working with mission partners "to formalize a process in the event UAP materiel is captured" - meaning that as of mid-2024 no such process existed anywhere the office could see. Section VI.C confirms that Oak Ridge National Laboratory analysed "a material claimed to be from a UAP." Section VI.D confirms AARO posted historical documents on KONA BLUE, a prospective and unapproved Special Access Program that interviewees described as a DHS compartment to cover up "the retrieval and exploitation of 'non-human biologics.'" And the report concedes that 444 of the 757 reports lacked sufficient data for any analysis and that AARO received zero UAP reporting through national GEOINT, SIGINT or MASINT platforms during the entire period - the national technical means that would find a concealed program were not pointed at the problem at all.

### The longer account

Read as a compliance document, the FY2024 report is a study in what a statutory reporting requirement can and cannot compel. 50 U.S.C. 3373(k)(1)(B) enumerates roughly fifteen mandatory content items, and the report walks them in order: events in period, events out of period, analysis by intelligence discipline, incidents over restricted airspace, threats, foreign attribution, breakthrough foreign capability, allied consultation, capture-or-exploitation efforts, health effects, nuclear-asset association, NNSA and NRC consultation, designated line organisations, and a summary of authorised reporting. The statute is well drafted. The answers are mostly negative, and the negatives are the interesting part.

The numbers: 757 reports received, of which 485 occurred in the reporting period and 272 occurred in 2021-2022 but were only reported now. Cumulative holdings as of 24 October 2024 were 1,652 reports. Of the 757, 392 - more than half - came from the FAA, and consisted of the entire FAA UAP reporting log since June 2021. By domain: 708 air, 49 space, zero maritime, zero transmedium; and AARO notes that none of the space-domain reports came from space-based sensors, all being pilot or ground-observer accounts of objects estimated above 100 kilometres. AARO resolved 49 cases in-period, recommended 243 more for closure, judged 21 to merit further analysis by intelligence-community and science-and-technology partners, and consigned 444 to an 'Active Archive' for lack of data. By publication, the office reported 118 resolved and a further 174 finalised, all to prosaic objects.

The key sentence for anyone tracking concealment is buried in Section III.B: "During the reporting period, AARO did not receive any UAP reports collected through national GEOINT, SIGINT, or MASINT platforms." Every input was pilot report, post-mission full-motion video, or FAA log. AARO can search IC databases case-by-case, but nothing was tasked. Whatever else the report establishes, it establishes that the collection architecture of the United States was not being used to answer this question during the period covered.

The second key passage is the nuclear-infrastructure section. AARO received eighteen reports from the NNSA Administrator and the NRC Chairman regarding incidents near U.S. nuclear infrastructure, weapons and launch sites, all of which those agencies categorised as unmanned aircraft systems. Two involved incursions of 53 minutes and one hour 57 minutes. On 3 August 2023 security at the D.C. Cook Nuclear Power Plant recovered a crashed UAS and turned it over to Berrien County, Michigan local law enforcement; AARO states flatly that it "has no further information about the crashed UAS." A recovered object over a nuclear facility left the federal system entirely and the office statutorily charged with anomaly resolution never got it back. Separately, the BWXT Fuel Cycle Facility in Lynchburg, Virginia experienced UAS overflights on six consecutive nights, 10-15 October 2023.

The third is the authorities language. AARO asserts it "is authorized by law to receive all UAP related information including any classified national security information involving military intelligence or intelligence related activities at all levels of classification, regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented special access programs," with "no restriction on AARO receiving any past or present UAP-related information, regardless of the organizational affiliation of the original classification authority." This is the exact authority whose absence is the subject of the Wilson-Davis memo - a Deputy Director of DIA being told by a contractor committee that his tickets and statutory authority were not sufficient. The FY2024 report asserts the authority exists. It does not report ever having exercised it against a waived or unacknowledged SAP, nor does it report a single instance of an access denial.

The science-and-technology section names the partner set: Oak Ridge National Laboratory for physics-relevant case analysis and the UAP-material analysis; the Air Force Research Laboratory for propulsion, stealth and sensing assessments; the Georgia Tech Research Institute, which built the GREMLIN sensor architecture that demonstrated functionality in a March 2024 test and was slated for a 90-day pattern-of-life collection "at a site of national security"; MIT Lincoln Laboratory, building prototype processing to recover objects currently filtered out of FAA and National Weather Service radar; and NASA as focal point for academic and open-source work. That is a real, funded, technically competent architecture, and its existence should be weighed against the report's own admission that the office cannot resolve most of its caseload.

Finally, the strategic communications section records that AARO posted historical documents relating to KONA BLUE, characterising it as "a Prospective and not approved Special Access Program that was brought to AARO's attention by interviewees who claimed that it was a sensitive Department of Homeland Security compartment to cover up the retrieval and exploitation of 'non-human biologics.'" AARO's framing is dismissive. But the disclosure itself confirms that a UAP-linked prospective SAP was drafted, staffed toward DHS, and documented well enough that its papers could be posted eight months later.

### What it has denied

Categorical and repeated: no evidence of extraterrestrial beings, activity or technology; no data indicating the capture or exploitation of UAP; no confirmation of foreign adversarial attribution. The denials are consistently framed as statements about what AARO possesses or has discovered rather than about what exists, which preserves deniability if material is held in a compartment AARO has not in fact penetrated.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- What the 21 cases judged to merit further analysis actually are, and what became of them
- Whether AARO has ever exercised its asserted authority to compel access to a waived or unacknowledged special access program, and whether any access was refused
- Which material was analysed by ORNL, who supplied it, and what the chain of custody was
- Why no national GEOINT, SIGINT or MASINT collection was tasked against UAP during a full reporting year, and who makes that tasking decision
- What happened to the UAS recovered at D.C. Cook Nuclear Power Plant on 3 August 2023 after it was handed to county law enforcement
- The identity of the 'site of national security' selected for the GREMLIN 90-day pattern-of-life collection
- Whether the report's zero maritime and zero transmedium counts reflect absence of events or absence of a reporting channel from the naval and undersea domains

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 90 of 1138, score 68). 8 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C0/A2/D2.

## Congressional UAP Caucus

Congressional UAP Caucus is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates 2023; axes C0 A1 D3; 8 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: Bipartisan House member organisation, est. Aug 2023.

### What it is

A bipartisan House caucus launched by Rep. Tim Burchett (TN-02) in August 2023 and co-chaired with Rep. Jared Moskowitz (FL-23). Its founding cohort, the six members who signed its first act, a letter to the Inspector General of the Intelligence Community, were Burchett, Moskowitz, Anna Paulina Luna (FL-13), Nancy Mace (SC-01), Eric Burlison (MO-07) and Andy Ogles (TN-05).

### Why it is implicated

The caucus is the only organised bloc in Congress whose founding premise is that UAP crash retrieval and reverse-engineering programs exist and have been concealed from the committees of jurisdiction. Its very first act, less than a month after David Grusch's July 2023 testimony, was to demand from the IC Inspector General the names of the people and the identities of the facilities involved in those programs. Its members subsequently drove the FY2024 NDAA's UAP Records Collection at the National Archives, the House Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, the September 2025 whistleblower hearing, and the UAP Disclosure Act amendment adopted into the House FY2027 NDAA on 22 July 2026 with its eminent domain provision intact, a provision that would order the government to seize recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence held by private entities. In August 2026 the relationship inverted: the executive branch authorised Burlison's House office to publish ODNI memo ES 2026-00818, making the oversight bloc the administration's distribution channel for its own IC directive.

### The longer account

On 26 July 2023 the House Oversight Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs held the first public UAP hearing to take testimony from military pilots and whistleblowers. David Grusch, a former intelligence officer, testified that he could not provide specific details regarding UAP crash retrieval or reverse engineering programs because those details are classified, but that he had provided them to the Office of the Inspector General of the Intelligence Community. Twenty-seven days later, on 22 August 2023, Tim Burchett announced that he had launched the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Caucus and had led five colleagues in a letter to IC IG Thomas A. Monheim asking for follow-up on Grusch's testimony, specifically, in the caucus's own words, "information from the Intelligence Community Inspector General regarding which people and facilities are involved with UAP crash retrieval programs and reverse engineering programs."

That question has never been publicly answered. The caucus's subsequent trajectory is best read as successive attempts to answer it by other means.

The statutory route came first. The FY2022 NDAA (Pub. L. 117-81, § 1683, codified at 50 U.S.C. § 3373) created AARO. The FY2023 NDAA (Pub. L. 117-263, § 1673, codified at 50 U.S.C. § 3373b) required a secure channel for authorised disclosure of UAP information relating to any government or contractor activity, notwithstanding NDAs, EO 13526, the Atomic Energy Act and Section 798 of the Espionage Act, and prohibited retaliation by agencies and by contractors, subcontractors, grantees and subgrantees. The FY2024 NDAA (Pub. L. 118-31, § 1841) then created the UAP Records Collection at the National Archives, defining covered records as any "Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence." That statutory phrase, "technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence", is now U.S. public law, drafted by a Congress that would not have needed the category if nothing occupied it.

The FY2024 NDAA also contained the sharpest instrument the caucus era produced and the one that quietly died: sections 1687 and 7343 prohibited the use of FY2024 funds for any UAP-related secret program until the Secretary of Defense or the Director of National Intelligence fully disclosed the relevant details to the appropriate congressional committees and leadership. Those funding limitations were not renewed in subsequent enactments. Whoever allowed that lapse removed the only appropriations-side leverage Congress had ever applied to the question.

The Senate route was the UAP Disclosure Act, which passed the Senate in 2023 as Division G of S. 2226 by unanimous-consent incorporation of Amendment No. 935 and would have created an independent review board empowered to advise the President on declassification and release of UAP records. Its most aggressive clause orders the government to "exercise eminent domain over any and all recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence that may be controlled by private persons or entities in the interests of the public good." It was stripped in conference. On 22 July 2026 the House adopted its own version, including the eminent domain provision, as an amendment to the FY2027 NDAA, recorded as Amendment No. 315 at H.R. Rep. 119-755, pages 419-442. The named sponsors are Burlison of Missouri, Crane of Arizona, Carson of Indiana, Luna of Florida, Moskowitz of Florida and Begich of Alaska: three of the six are caucus founders, and Crane, Carson and Begich extend the coalition. The House bloc carried into law-adjacent text the provision the Senate had been unable to protect.

The hearing route continued through the 119th Congress. Luna chairs the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, which held a hearing on 9 September 2025 titled "Restoring Public Trust Through UAP Transparency and Whistleblower Protection," taking testimony from Air Force veteran Jeffrey Nuccetelli, Chief Alexandro Wiggins, journalist George Knapp and Air Force veteran Dylan Borland. That task force also runs the JFK files declassification work, the same members, the same theory of the case, applied to two record sets.

The most revealing development is procedural rather than legislative. On 21 July 2026 Burlison publicly invited anyone covered by President Trump's UAP NDA directive to contact his office for help navigating the process, a member of Congress standing up an informal whistleblower intake because the official one did not yet exist. Eleven days later, ODNI signed memorandum ES 2026-00818. On 1 August 2026 Burlison released it, noting that "the administration authorized Congressman Burlison's office to publicly distribute the memorandum." A national-level Intelligence Community directive to the Director of the CIA and the heads of every IC element reached the public through the website of a backbench House member from southwest Missouri who had spent three years accusing that same community of concealment. Whether that is co-option or capitulation is the open question; either way it is the clearest measure of the caucus's leverage in 2026. It is also worth noting what the memo Burlison distributed does not give him: it forecloses disclosure to Congress outside AARO, meaning the caucus published an instrument that routes around itself.

One cataloguing caution for downstream analysis: the domain uapcaucus.com belongs to a private advocacy organisation, not to the congressional caucus, and the House caucus has never published an official membership roster.

### What it has denied

The caucus issues no denials; it exists to contest them. Its own weak point is documented rather than asserted: it has never published an answer from the IC Inspector General to its founding question about the people and facilities involved in crash retrieval and reverse-engineering programs, and the FY2024 funding prohibition on undisclosed UAP programs, the only appropriations lever ever applied, was allowed to lapse without public explanation.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Did the Intelligence Community Inspector General ever answer the 22 August 2023 letter, and has any response been provided to the signatories in classified session?
- The House caucus has never published an official membership roster; current membership and its growth since 2023 are undocumented. Note that uapcaucus.com is a private advocacy organisation, not the congressional caucus.
- Who removed the FY2024 UAP secret-program funding prohibition from subsequent authorisation bills, and at whose request?
- Will the FY2027 UAPDA amendment, including eminent domain, survive conference with the Senate?
- Has the eminent domain provision been reviewed by the Office of Legal Counsel or scored for takings liability under the Fifth Amendment?
- Which private entities would be covered by the eminent domain provision, and has any member identified them by name in classified session?
- Why did the administration choose a caucus member's office as the release channel for ES 2026-00818, and was any condition attached?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 646 of 1138, score 26). 8 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

### Its strongest connections

# NO DATE: no start date recorded

## Waived SAP Notification Group

Waived SAP Notification Group is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates no dates recorded; axes C3 A1 D3; 30 connections; in the oversight band.

In one line: The persons entitled to notice of, and access to, a waived DoD special access program.

### What it is

10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) - the chairman and ranking minority member of each defense committee; twelve members, not the Gang of Eight

### Why it is implicated

The group that receives notification of a WAIVED Department of Defense special access program is not the Gang of Eight, and conflating the two is the single most common error in public argument about how a legacy programme could be hidden. The waived-SAP channel is created by 10 U.S.C. 119(e). Subsection (e)(1) provides: 'The Secretary of Defense may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report under that subsection if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security. Any such waiver shall be made on a case-by-case basis.' Subsection (e)(2) then specifies where the withheld material goes instead: 'If the Secretary exercises the authority provided under paragraph (1), the Secretary shall provide the information described in that subsection with respect to the s

### The longer account

The group that receives notification of a WAIVED Department of Defense special access program is not the Gang of Eight, and conflating the two is the single most common error in public argument about how a legacy programme could be hidden. The waived-SAP channel is created by 10 U.S.C. 119(e). Subsection (e)(1) provides: 'The Secretary of Defense may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report under that subsection if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security. Any such waiver shall be made on a case-by-case basis.' Subsection (e)(2) then specifies where the withheld material goes instead: 'If the Secretary exercises the authority provided under paragraph (1), the Secretary shall provide the information described in that subsection with respect to the special access program concerned, and the justification for the waiver, jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' The term 'defense committees' is defined not by the general Title 10 definition but by section 119's own subsection (g): 'the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations, and the Defense Subcommittee of the Committee on Appropriations, of the Senate' and 'the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations, and the Subcommittee on Defense of the Committee on Appropriations, of the House of Representatives.' Six bodies, each contributing a chairman and a ranking minority member: twelve members of Congress. This is deliberately narrower than a full committee and deliberately wider than eight. Note that 10 U.S.C. 101(a)(16) defines a different, similar-sounding term, 'congressional defense committees,' as only the Armed Services and Appropriations Committees of the two chambers - four bodies, eight chairs and rankings. Section 119 does not use that definition; it uses its own, which adds the two Defense Appropriations Subcommittees. A board that cites 101(a)(16) for the waived-SAP group would be naming the wrong set. The Gang of Eight is a separate creature governing covert action, not DoD SAPs. Under 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2), where the President determines that it is essential to limit access to a covert action finding to meet extraordinary circumstances affecting vital interests of the United States, 'the finding may be reported to the chairmen and ranking minority members of the congressional intelligence committees, the Speaker and minority leader of the House of Representatives, the majority and minority leaders of the Senate, and such other member or members of the congressional leadership as may be included by the President.' Different committees (intelligence, not armed services or appropriations), different people (the four party leaders sit in the Gang of Eight and are absent from the 119(e) group; the appropriators sit in the 119(e) group and are absent from the Gang of Eight), and different subject matter. The structural comparison is where the argument actually lives, and it cuts against the covert-action statute being the tighter one. Title 50 contains two escape valves that Title 10 does not. First, 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(5)(B) imposes a 180-day clock: within 180 days of a statement of reasons for limiting access, the President must either give all members of the intelligence committees access to the finding or submit a fresh statement of reasons for continuing to withhold. Section 119(e) contains no sunset, no periodic re-justification, and no mechanism that ever widens the circle. A waiver, once made, is permanent as to that report. Second, 50 U.S.C. 3093(g)(1)-(2) requires the President to tell all members of the intelligence committee that a limited notification occurred and to give them a general description of it. Section 119 has no analogue: the members of the Armed Services and Appropriations Committees who are not chair or ranking are never told that a waiver happened at all. They cannot ask about what they do not know exists. That asymmetry - no sunset, no notice of bypass - is the load-bearing limit on congressional oversight of a waived programme, and it is a limit written into the statute rather than an abuse of it. What the twelve can see is also bounded. They receive the withheld report content and the Secretary's justification. They do not thereby acquire subpoena power over the programme, staff who can read the material, or any statutory right to share it with colleagues. What they cannot see is anything the Department declines to characterise as a DoD special access program at all - the recurring allegation in the UAP context being that recovery and reverse-engineering activity was moved into contractor-held or non-DoD structures where section 119 does not reach. Finally, the mechanism's present scope is narrower than it was. Pub. L. 114-328 section 1061(c)(4) lists 'Section 119(a) and (b)' among the reporting requirements it terminates, and section 1061(b)(1) provides that each such report still required as of December 31, 2021 'shall no longer be required to be submitted to Congress after that date.' The Office of the Law Revision Counsel carries this as a Termination of Reporting Requirements note under section 119. The annual SAP budget report and the annual new-SAP designation-and-justification report are therefore gone. Because 119(e)(1) can only waive a requirement 'under subsection (a), (b), or (c),' the waiver authority now has live operation only as to subsection (c), the report on a planned change in classification or a planned declassification and public announcement. Subsections (d), (f) and (g) survive and are not waivable at all - notably 119(f), which bars initiating any SAP until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Department of Defense continues in practice to provide SAP budget and new-programme information to the defense committees after the 31 December 2021 termination of 10 U.S.C. 119(a) and (b) - for example under classified annex direction in annual authorisation or appropriations acts, or voluntarily - and whether the 119(e)(2) chair-and-ranking channel is still used for that material. This could not be established from statutory text and would require the classified annex language or committee report language to resolve.
- Whether any waiver under 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(1) has ever been exercised in connection with UAP-related activity. No public record establishes this either way, and the statute creates no public reporting of the fact that a waiver occurred.
- The precise date on which Howard P. 'Buck' McKeon replaced John M. McHugh as the senior Republican on the House Armed Services Committee in 2009. The December 2009 Congressional Directory shows McKeon in the seat and army.mil records McHugh taking office as Secretary of the Army in 2009, but the exact date of the House Armed Services succession was not confirmed from a primary source. For any claim tied to a specific 2009 date, verify which of the two held the ranking seat on that date.
- The name of the 2009 Senate Appropriations Defense Subcommittee ranking minority member is taken from the first-listed Republican in the 111th Congress committee assignment publication rather than from an explicit 'ranking member' designation. The publication marks chairmen explicitly but identifies minority seniority only by list order. The reading is that Thad Cochran held it, consistent with his position as the full committee's senior Republican, but the publication does not label it.
- Whether the September 2024 reissuance of DoDD 5205.07 formally retired the acknowledged / unacknowledged / waived taxonomy or merely omitted it from the directive while retaining it in the subordinate instruction DoDI 5205.11, which was reissued the same day and was not retrieved. This matters for how the board should label the waived-usap node.
- Whether Tom Cole's tenure as House Appropriations chairman began before the 119th Congress. The sources retrieved confirm him in the chair as of June 2026 but do not establish a start date.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 23 of 1138, score 103). 31 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A1/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 101(a)(16) defines the different term 'congressional defense committees' as only '(A) the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations of the Senate; and (B) the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations of the House of Representatives' - four bodies, not the six used by section 119. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section101&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) directs waived-SAP information and the waiver justification 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees' - twelve members, given the six bodies listed in 119(g). Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(f) bars initiating a special access program until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse. Subsection (f) is outside the reach of the 119(e)(1) waiver, which by its terms covers only subsections (a), (b) and (c). Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(g) defines 'defense committees' for this section as the Senate Armed Services Committee, Senate Appropriations Committee, Senate Appropriations Defense Subcommittee, House Armed Services Committee, House Appropriations Committee, and House Appropriations Defense Subcommittee. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim

Collins; SAC Defense Subcommittee Chairman Jon Tester, Ranking Member Susan M. Collins. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GPO-CPUB-118spub3/html/GPO-CPUB-118spub3.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(5)(B) forces re-justification or widened access within 180 days of a limited covert-action notification. 10 U.S.C. 119(e) contains no sunset and no re-justification requirement, so a SAP reporting waiver is permanent as to that report. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3093(g)(1)-(2) requires that all members of the intelligence committee be told a limited notification occurred and be given a general description of it. Section 119 imposes no equivalent duty, so non-leadership members of the defense committees are never informed that a waiver has been exercised. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim

Coons (SAC Defense Subcommittee Ranking Member); Mike Rogers of Alabama (HASC Chairman) and Adam Smith (HASC Ranking Member); Tom Cole (HAC Chairman) and Rosa L. DeLauro (HAC Ranking Member); Ken Calvert (HAC Defense Subcommittee Chair) and Betty McCollum (HAC Defense Subcommittee Ranking Member). Source: https://www.armed-services.senate.gov/about/membership
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Directive 5205.07, reissued 12 September 2024, sets DoD special access program policy and its glossary defines SAP, SAP umbrella, SAP compartment, Joint Force Integration SAP and Strategic Enabler SAP. It does not define or use the terms 'acknowledged', 'unacknowledged' or 'waived' SAP, which appeared in the superseded July 2010 version. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 para. 2.1.e assigns the Director, DoD SAPCO responsibility to accredit congressional SAP facilities and to maintain records of DoD SAP information provided to Congress. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 para. 3.1.g requires all personnel accessed to DoD SAPs to notify the Director, DoD SAPCO through their cognizant authority SAPCO when briefing or providing DoD SAP material to any member of Congress or their staff, and bars any DoD contractor entity, employee, representative or consultant from providing SAP material to members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO's own access is gated through the same two seats. DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.g provides that GAO access runs through 'the chairman and ranking defense committee members, unless the GAO is already tasked with a SAP-related action in existing legislation.' This is the structural reason GAO's published special access program work has never been able to report on the waiver's use. Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Professional staff members are cut out of waived programs entirely. DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.c.(1) reads verbatim: 'One professional staff member for both the majority and the minority party of the defense and intelligence committees may be granted access to all non-waived SAPs within the respective committee's SAP oversight jurisdiction.' Paragraph 7.2.d applies the same limit to non-committee members: 'Members of Congress and professional staff members not assigned to the defense or intelligence committees may be granted access to non-waived DoD SAPs by the Director, DoD SAPCO following consultation with the respective chamber of Congress chair and ranking defense committee members.' Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Pub. L. 114-328 sec. 1061(c)(4) lists 'Section 119(a) and (b)' among terminated reporting requirements, and sec. 1061(b)(1) ends them after 31 December 2021. The OLRC note under section 119 records the termination as effective 31 December 2021. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section111&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE BUDGET PAPER FOR A WAIVED PROGRAM GOES TO STAFF, NOT TO MEMBERS. DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.h reads verbatim: 'The DoD will conform to all congressional reporting requirements. The DoD will submit to the congressional defense and intelligence committees such additional materials as are requested by the committees and are within the jurisdiction of each committee. Justification materials for waived programs will be presented only to the committee staff directors.' This is a distinct and narrower channel than the 119(e)(2) delivery to chairs and ranking members, and it means the written justification material for a waived program is routed to two staff directors per committee rather than to the twelve statutory seats. Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE STATUTORY GROUP AND THE DEPARTMENT'S GROUP ARE NOT THE SAME, AND THE DEPARTMENT'S IS WIDER. The statute, 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2), names only 'the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees' - twelve seats across six committee entities, and no staff. DoD Instruction 5205.11, paragraph 7.2.a.(3), states verbatim: 'Only the chair, the ranking member, the staff directors, and one designated security manager of the defense and intelligence committees will be authorized access to waived DoD SAPs within their committee's respective oversight jurisdiction. Requests to access additional members of Congress or professional staff must be approved by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense.' The Department therefore adds two staff categories the statute does not name, and extends the group to the two intelligence committees, which Section 119(g) does not include. Whether that is a lawful gloss or an administrative expansion is a live question the board can put on the record. Source: Office of the Law Revision Counsel, 10 U.S.C. 119, 'Special access programs: congressional oversight' (text in effect on 20 August 2026) - https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim ; and DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The December 2009 Congressional Directory records Ike Skelton as HASC Chairman and Howard P. 'Buck' McKeon as senior Republican, David R. Obey as HAC Chairman with Jerry Lewis senior Republican, and John P. Murtha as HAC Defense Subcommittee Chairman with C. W. Bill Young senior Republican. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CDIR-2009-12-01/html/CDIR-2009-12-01-HOUSECOMMITTEES.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Gang of Eight under 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2) consists of 'the chairmen and ranking minority members of the congressional intelligence committees, the Speaker and minority leader of the House of Representatives, the majority and minority leaders of the Senate, and such other member or members of the congressional leadership as may be included by the President.' It governs covert action findings, not DoD special access programs. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** The exclusion of ordinary committee members from waived programs is stated flatly. DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.b reads verbatim: 'Members of Congress assigned to the defense and intelligence committees are authorized access to all DoD SAPs within the respective committee's SAP oversight jurisdiction, except for waived programs as described previously. The intelligence committees are authorized access only to those DoD SAPs designated by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense as intelligence or intelligence related.' Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The historical practice of the notification was oral. The Moynihan Commission recorded in 1997 that for waived programs 'the chairperson, ranking member, and, on occasion, other members and staff of relevant Congressional committees are notified only orally of the existence of these programs.' If that practice persisted, then the 119(e)(2) delivery of 'the information ... and the justification for the waiver' generates no artefact that can later be produced, subpoenaed, archived or discovered - which is the single most consequential fact on this node. Source: Report of the Commission on Protecting and Reducing Government Secrecy (the Moynihan Commission), S. Doc. 105-2, 1997, Chapter II, as mirrored by the Federation of American Scientists - https://sgp.fas.org/library/moynihan/chap2.html

A further 4 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

### Its strongest connections

- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **United States Department of Defense (secondary designation: Department of War)** (institution, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Secretary of Defense is the notifying authority under 119(e). The waiver decision under 119(e)(1) belongs to the Secretary of Defense personally and must be made case by case on a determination that inclusion would adversely affect the national security. The statute prescribes no review of that determination. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; Archived: Department of War (Department of Defense) - About (Wayback 2026-08-10), https://web.archive.org/web/20260810202005/https://www.war.gov/About/, dated 2026-08-10.
- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **Eric Burlison** (person, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: no statutory entitlement; not a defense committee chair or ranking member. Burlison is not listed among the members of the House Armed Services Committee and does not hold a chair or ranking seat on any of the six bodies named in 10 U.S.C. 119(g). His UAP oversight work therefore proceeds without any section 119 entitlement. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. Senate, Dates of Sessions of the Congress (convening dates used to date each seat), https://www.senate.gov/legislative/DatesofSessionsofCongress.htm, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. GPO, Hearing transcript, Serial No. 118-53 (CHRG-118hhrg53022), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-118hhrg53022/html/CHRG-118hhrg53022.htm, dated 2023-07-26; https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm, https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm
- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **Anna Paulina Luna** (person, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: no statutory entitlement; not a defense committee chair or ranking member. Luna is not listed among the members of the House Armed Services Committee and does not hold a chair or ranking seat on any of the six bodies named in 10 U.S.C. 119(g). Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. Senate, Dates of Sessions of the Congress (convening dates used to date each seat), https://www.senate.gov/legislative/DatesofSessionsofCongress.htm, dated 2026-08-22; Archived: Department of War - PURSUE portal (war.gov/UFO) (Wayback 2026-08-10), https://web.archive.org/web/20260810202017/https://www.war.gov/UFO/, dated 2026-08-10; https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm, https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm
- Waived SAP Notification Group runs out to **Waived unacknowledged special access program (waived USAP)** (program, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: sole statutory recipient of waived-SAP notification. The waiver mechanism at 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(1) does not eliminate reporting; it redirects it. Whatever the Secretary withholds from the report goes instead to these twelve members under 119(e)(2), together with the justification for withholding. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; 10 U.S.C. 119, Special access programs: congressional oversight (full text, U.S. Code 2023 Edition, GPO), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm, dated 2024-01-05.
- Waived SAP Notification Group runs out to **Waived unacknowledged special access program (waived USAP)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: the twelve members with statutory sight of a waived programme. Entitlement is bounded by characterisation: section 119 reaches only activity the Department treats as a DoD special access program. Activity held outside that characterisation is outside the group's statutory sight line entirely. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; Archived: DoD Directive 5205.07, Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (Wayback 2026-05-18), https://web.archive.org/web/20260518065512/https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2026-05-18.
- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **DoD Special Access Program Central Office (DoD SAPCO)** (institution, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: DoDI 5205.11 para. 7.2.a(3) - waived-SAP access limited to chair, ranking member, staff directors and one security manager. DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs,' effective 12 September 2024, para. 7.2.a(3): 'Only the chair, the ranking member, the staff directors, and one designated security manager of the defense and intelligence committees will be authorized access to waived DoD SAPs within their committee's respective oversight jurisdiction. Requests to access additional members of Congress or professional staff must be approved by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense.' This is materially wider than 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) on two axes: it admits committee staff directors and a security manager, and it extends to the intelligence committees, which have no entitlement under section 119. Any board text asserting that the twelve have no staff who can read the material should be corrected against this paragraph. Para. 7.2.b confirms that ordinary members of the defense and intelligence committees are authorised access to all DoD SAPs in their jurisdiction 'except for waived programs.' Sources: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf; 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; Archived: DoD Directive 5205.07, Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (Wayback 2026-05-18), https://web.archive.org/web/20260518065512/https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2026-05-18; https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **Harry M. Reid** (person, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Senate Majority Leader in 2009 - in the Gang of Eight, not in the 119(e)(2) group. This is the cleanest demonstration that the two small groups are different sets. 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2) includes the majority and minority leaders of the Senate. 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) and 119(g) do not; they reach only committee and subcommittee chairs and ranking members. A Senate Majority Leader has no statutory entitlement to waived DoD SAP notification by virtue of that office. In 2009 the Senate armed services and appropriations seats were held by Levin, McCain, Inouye and Cochran. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2), the Gang of Eight construct, for contrast with the section 119 group, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. Senate, Dates of Sessions of the Congress (convening dates used to date each seat), https://www.senate.gov/legislative/DatesofSessionsofCongress.htm, dated 2026-08-22; Letter, Sen. Harry Reid to Deputy Secretary of Defense William J. Lynn III, 24 June 2009 (DIA FOIA release), https://www.dia.mil/FOIA/FOIA-Electronic-Reading-Room/FileId/237659/, dated 2009-06-24; https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GPO-CPUB-111spub13/html/GPO-CPUB-111spub13.htm, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GPO-CPUB-111spub13/html/GPO-CPUB-111spub13.htm
- Waived SAP Notification Group runs out to **10 U.S.C. 119 - Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight** (legislation, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: created by 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) and defined by 119(g). Section 119 was added by Pub. L. 100-180 div. A title XI sec. 1132(a)(1), 4 December 1987. Subsection (e)(2) directs waived-SAP information and the waiver justification jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each defense committee; subsection (g) supplies the six-body definition of 'defense committees' used by the section. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; 10 U.S.C. 119, Special access programs: congressional oversight (full text, U.S. Code 2023 Edition, GPO), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm, dated 2024-01-05.
- Waived SAP Notification Group comes in from **Nancy Ruth Mace** (person, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: House Armed Services member but not chair or ranking - no 119(e)(2) entitlement. Mace sits on the House Armed Services Committee, one of the six defense committees, yet receives nothing under section 119(e)(2), which names only the chairman and ranking minority member. She would also not be told under section 119 that a waiver had been exercised at all. This is the clearest single illustration of how narrow the aperture is even inside a defense committee. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. Senate, Dates of Sessions of the Congress (convening dates used to date each seat), https://www.senate.gov/legislative/DatesofSessionsofCongress.htm, dated 2026-08-22; CHRG-118hhrg57440, printed hearing transcript, 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Exposing the Truth', Serial No. 118-135, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-118hhrg57440/pdf/CHRG-118hhrg57440.pdf, dated 2024-11-13; https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm, https://armedservices.house.gov/about/members.htm
- Waived SAP Notification Group runs out to **U.S. House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform** (institution, board tier B), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: not a defense committee; receives nothing under 10 U.S.C. 119. The July 2023 Grusch testimony was taken by a House Oversight subcommittee. That committee has no entitlement under section 119(e)(2) and no statutory route to compel waived-SAP material, which is a structural reason why the hearing could establish allegation but not access. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; U.S. GPO, Hearing transcript, Serial No. 118-53 (CHRG-118hhrg53022), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-118hhrg53022/html/CHRG-118hhrg53022.htm, dated 2023-07-26.
- Waived SAP Notification Group runs out to **Congressional oversight of special access programs** (institution, board tier B), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: the narrow aperture through which Congress sees waived DoD SAPs. Section 119 contains no analogue to 50 U.S.C. 3093(g), which requires that the rest of the committee be told a limited notification occurred. Members of the defense committees outside the twelve are not informed that a waiver has been exercised. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; Archived: DoD Directive 5205.07, Special Access Program (SAP) Policy (Wayback 2026-05-18), https://web.archive.org/web/20260518065512/https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf, dated 2026-05-18; https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3093&num=0&edition=prelim
